1   Snow hydrology in Mediterranean mountain regions: a review [SOURCE: Page_3]

2   Abbas Fayad^1,2,*, Simon Gascoin^1, Ghaleb Faour^2, Juan Ignacio López-Moreno^3, Laurent Drapeau^1, [SOURCE: Page_3]

3   Michel Le Page^1, and Richard Escadafal^1 [SOURCE: Page_3]

4 1 5       Centre d’Etudes Spatiales de la Biosphère (CESBIO), UPS/CNRS/IRD/CNES, Toulouse, France 2 6       National Council for Scientific Research/Remote Sensing Center (CNRS/RSC), Beirut, Lebanon 3 7       Pyrenean Institute of Ecology (CSIC), Department of Geoenvironmental Processes and Global [SOURCE: Page_3]

10   Abstract [SOURCE: Page_3]

11   Water resources in Mediterranean regions are under increasing pressure due to climate change, [SOURCE: Page_3]

12   economic development, and population growth. Many Mediterranean rivers have their headwaters in [SOURCE: Page_3]

13   mountainous regions where hydrological processes are driven by snowpack dynamics and the specific [SOURCE: Page_3]

14   variability of the Mediterranean climate. A good knowledge of the snow processes in the [SOURCE: Page_3]

15   Mediterranean mountains is therefore a key element of water management strategies in such regions. [SOURCE: Page_3]

16   The objective of this paper is to review the literature on snow hydrology in Mediterranean mountains [SOURCE: Page_3]

17   to identify the existing knowledge, key research questions, and promising technologies. We collected [SOURCE: Page_3]

18   620 peer-reviewed papers, published between 1913 and 2016, that deal with the Mediterranean-like [SOURCE: Page_3]

19   mountain regions in the western United States, the central Chilean Andes, and the Mediterranean [SOURCE: Page_3]

20   basin. We find a large amount of studies in the western United States that form a strong scientific [SOURCE: Page_3]

21   basis for other Mediterranean mountain regions. We found that: (1) the persistence of snow cover is [SOURCE: Page_3]

22   highly variable in space and time but mainly controlled by elevation and precipitation; (2) the [SOURCE: Page_3]

23   snowmelt is driven by radiative fluxes, but the contribution of heat fluxes is stronger at the end of the [SOURCE: Page_3]

24   snow season and during heat waves and rain-on-snow events; (3) the snow densification rates are [SOURCE: Page_3]

25   higher in these regions when compared to other climate regions; and (4) the snow sublimation is an [SOURCE: Page_3]

26   important component of snow ablation, especially in high-elevation regions. Among the pressing [SOURCE: Page_3]

1 [SOURCE: Page_3]

27   issues is the lack of continuous ground observation in high-elevation regions. However, a few years of [SOURCE: Page_4]

28   snow depth (HS) and snow water equivalent (SWE) data can provide realistic information on [SOURCE: Page_4]

29   snowpack variability. A better spatial characterization of snow cover can be achieved by combining [SOURCE: Page_4]

30   ground observations with remotely sensed snow data. SWE reconstruction using satellite snow cover [SOURCE: Page_4]

31   area and a melt model provides reasonable information that is suitable for hydrological applications. [SOURCE: Page_4]

32   Further advances in our understanding of the snow processes in Mediterranean snow-dominated [SOURCE: Page_4]

33   basins will be achieved by finer and more accurate representation of the climate forcing. While the [SOURCE: Page_4]

34   theory on the snowpack energy and mass balance is now well established, the connections between [SOURCE: Page_4]

35   the snow cover and the water resources involve complex interactions with the sub-surface processes, [SOURCE: Page_4]

36   which demand future investigation. [SOURCE: Page_4]

37   Keywords: Snowpack; Snow hydrology; Mountain hydrology, Mediterranean climate [SOURCE: Page_4]

38 [SOURCE: Page_4]

39   1. Introduction [SOURCE: Page_4]

40           Mountain regions are a major source of surface water and groundwater recharge in the [SOURCE: Page_4]

41   world (Viviroli et al., 2007; Dettinger, 2014). The water balance in mountainous regions is [SOURCE: Page_4]

42   defined by the interactions between the climate, cryospheric, and hydrological systems (de [SOURCE: Page_4]

43   Jong et al. eds. 2005; DeWalle and Rango, 2008). In mountainous regions that are under the [SOURCE: Page_4]

44   influence of the Mediterranean climate (Bolle 2003; Lionello et al. 2006), the wet-winters [SOURCE: Page_4]

45   and hot and dry-summers climate, orographic enhanced precipitation, variability of [SOURCE: Page_4]

46   temperature and the partitioning of rain and snow with elevation, and the high seasonal [SOURCE: Page_4]

47   variability of the snow cover make the hydrologic processes in these mountainous regions [SOURCE: Page_4]

48   significantly different from those found in other cryospheric regions and or dry or wet [SOURCE: Page_4]

49   climates. Under the influence of the Mediterranean climate, an important fraction of the [SOURCE: Page_4]

50   precipitation occurs during winter months (e.g., Demaria et al., 2013a; López-Moreno et al., [SOURCE: Page_4]

51   2013a; Valdés-Pineda et al., 2014), with the highest elevation areas receiving most of this [SOURCE: Page_4]

2 [SOURCE: Page_4]

52   winter precipitation as snow while the mid-elevation areas have a mixed precipitation regime [SOURCE: Page_5]

53   (McCabe et al., 2007; Surfleet and Tullos, 2013; Guan et al., 2016). Winter precipitation is [SOURCE: Page_5]

54   orographically enhanced along with elevation (Dettinger et al., 2004; Behrangi et al., 2016; [SOURCE: Page_5]

55   Derin et al., 2016). The snowmelt from the Mediterranean mountain occurs in the spring and [SOURCE: Page_5]

56   summer when precipitation is otherwise scarce, and thus, this snowmelt is an essential water [SOURCE: Page_5]

57   resource for many communities living in the surrounding low land regions (Morán-Tejeda et [SOURCE: Page_5]

58   al., 2010; López-Moreno et al., 2008a, 2014). [SOURCE: Page_5]

59          The Mediterranean mountain regions include the countries around the Mediterranean [SOURCE: Page_5]

60   Sea (e.g., Morocco, Spain, France, Italy, Bulgaria, Croatia, Greece, Turkey, and Lebanon), [SOURCE: Page_5]

61   the western US (California), and the mid-latitude area of the central Chilean Andes. In almost [SOURCE: Page_5]

62   all these regions, agriculture is an important source of income and employment, and [SOURCE: Page_5]

63   snowmelt provides runoff during the crop-growing season when irrigation is the most needed. [SOURCE: Page_5]

64   However, the sustainability of the water resources is threatened by the pressure of a growing [SOURCE: Page_5]

65   population, increasing irrigation, and climate change (e.g., Barnett et al., 2008). While [SOURCE: Page_5]

66   Mediterranean region have been considered as climate change “hot spots” since the first [SOURCE: Page_5]

67   IPCC reports (Milly et al., 2005; Giorgi, 2006; Nohara et al., 2006; Nogués-Bravo et al., [SOURCE: Page_5]

68   2007; Giorgi and Lionello, 2008; Loarie et al., 2009; Kyselý et al., 2012; Kapnick and [SOURCE: Page_5]

69   Delworth, 2013; Morán-Tejeda et al., 2014; Prudhomme et al., 2014; Harpold and Molotch, [SOURCE: Page_5]

70   2015; Vano et al., 2015; Kumar et al., 2016), there is also new evidence that the rate of [SOURCE: Page_5]

71   atmospheric warming increases with elevation (Kotlarski et al., 2015; Pepin et al., 2015), [SOURCE: Page_5]

72   which strengthens the concern about the climate change in Mediterranean mountain regions. [SOURCE: Page_5]

73   The impact of atmospheric warming is expected to be strong in snow-dominated watersheds [SOURCE: Page_5]

74   since snow accumulation and ablation are highly sensitive to air temperature (Beniston, 2003; [SOURCE: Page_5]

75   Barnett et al., 2005; Howat and Tulaczyk, 2005; Brown and Mote, 2009; Cooper et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_5]

76   The main consequence on warming is a shift in the hydrological regimes from a snow- [SOURCE: Page_5]

3 [SOURCE: Page_5]

77    dominated regime towards a rain-dominated regime (Berghuijs et al., 2014; Goulden and [SOURCE: Page_6]

78    Bales, 2014). For example, in the western US, areas with elevations between 2000 to 2800 m [SOURCE: Page_6]

79    are the most sensitive to global warming (Maurer et al., 2007). Regions in the western US [SOURCE: Page_6]

80    where the average winter-wet-day minimum temperature increased by +3 °C are witnessing a [SOURCE: Page_6]

81    reduction in the winter-total snowfall to precipitation ratio (Knowles et al., 2006). Most [SOURCE: Page_6]

82    Northern-Hemisphere’s snow-dependent regions are likely to experience increasing stress [SOURCE: Page_6]

83    from low snow years within the next three decades (Diffenbaugh et al., 2012). Areas with an [SOURCE: Page_6]

84    average winter temperature between -4 and -2 °C are expected to witness shifts towards [SOURCE: Page_6]

85    earlier streamflow peaks (changes that exceed 45 days relative to those from 1961–1990) [SOURCE: Page_6]

86    (Maurer et al., 2007). Similar results were reported in the Southern Alpine river basins [SOURCE: Page_6]

87    (Zampieri et al., 2015). The Mediterranean climate is also characterized by a high inter- [SOURCE: Page_6]

88    annual variability, although the underlying mechanisms depend upon the specific region. [SOURCE: Page_6]

89    North and South American Mediterranean regions are under the influence of the climatic [SOURCE: Page_6]

90    variability of the Pacific Ocean, while the Mediterranean climate in Europe and North Africa [SOURCE: Page_6]

91    is relatively connected to the North-Atlantic climatic variability (López-Moreno et al., [SOURCE: Page_6]

92    2013a). [SOURCE: Page_6]

93           Research on the seasonal snow and snow hydrology in mountains is well established [SOURCE: Page_6]

94    and several authors have already produced review articles or chapters on the following: (1) [SOURCE: Page_6]

95    the physical properties of the snowpack (e.g., Armstrong and Burn (eds) 2008; Kinar and [SOURCE: Page_6]

96    Pomeroy, 2015; Sturm, 2015); (2) remote sensing of snow (Dozier and Painter, 2004; Seidel [SOURCE: Page_6]

97    and Martinec 2004; Dozier et al., 2009; Dietz et al., 2012; Frei et al., 2012; Deems et al., [SOURCE: Page_6]

98    2013; Lettenmaier et al., 2015; Sturm, 2015; Tedesco (ed.) 2015); (3) spatial distribution of [SOURCE: Page_6]

99    the snow water equivalent, SWE (Dozier et al., 2016); (4) snow and mountain hydrology [SOURCE: Page_6]

100   (e.g., Seidel and Martinec 2004; de Jong et al. (eds.) 2005; Bales et al., 2006;; Armstrong and [SOURCE: Page_6]

101   Burn (eds) 2008; DeWalle and Rango 2008; Varhola et al., 2010; Hrachowitz et al., 2013; [SOURCE: Page_6]

4 [SOURCE: Page_6]

102   Bierkens, 2015; Sturm, 2015); (5) snow spatial representation in hydrologic and land-surface [SOURCE: Page_7]

103   models (e.g., Clark et al., 2011); and the (6) projected climate impact on the cryospheric and [SOURCE: Page_7]

104   hydrological systems in mountains (e.g., Beniston, 2003; de Jong et al. (eds.) 2005; Huber et [SOURCE: Page_7]

105   al. (eds.) 2005; Vicuña and Dracup, 2007; Viviroli et al., 2007; Brown and Mote, 2009; [SOURCE: Page_7]

106   Stewart, 2009; García-Ruiz et al., 2011; Diffenbaugh et al., 2012; Kapnick and Delworth, [SOURCE: Page_7]

107   2013; Beniston and Stoffel, 2014; Pepin et al., 2015; Sturm 2015; Wu et al., 2015; Meixner et [SOURCE: Page_7]

108   al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_7]

109           Snow processes are driven by meteorological forcing (energy and mass fluxes) and [SOURCE: Page_7]

110   land surface physiography (topography and vegetation). Therefore, the snowpack presents [SOURCE: Page_7]

111   different characteristics depending on the region. The standard snow classification that was [SOURCE: Page_7]

112   introduced by Sturm et al., (1995) defines seven classes of seasonal snow according to their [SOURCE: Page_7]

113   physical properties: Tundra, Taiga, Alpine, Maritime, Ephemeral, Prairie, or Mountain. The [SOURCE: Page_7]

114   “Mediterranean snow” can be observed as a subset of the Maritime class, and it is [SOURCE: Page_7]

115   characterized by a warmer snowpack, a shorter snow season, and a higher variance in both [SOURCE: Page_7]

116   the intra-annual snow depth and mean monthly snow density compared to the Tundra, Taiga, [SOURCE: Page_7]

117   and Prairie snow regions (Brown and Mote, 2009; Sturm et al., 2010; Bormann et al., 2013) [SOURCE: Page_7]

118   (Fig. 1). In comparison with the Tundra and Taiga regions, the snowpack in Mediterranean [SOURCE: Page_7]

119   mountains is characterized by higher mean annual snow densities and higher annual [SOURCE: Page_7]

120   densification rates (Bormann et al., 2013; Trujillo and Molotch, 2014) (Fig. 1). [SOURCE: Page_7]

121           To our knowledge, no review to date has explicitly addressed the characterization of [SOURCE: Page_7]

122   snow hydrologic processes related to snow in one of the major snow regions. Given the [SOURCE: Page_7]

123   significance of the snowmelt as a water resource in Mediterranean regions, we considered [SOURCE: Page_7]

124   that a review of the previous snow studies undertaken in Mediterranean mountain regions is [SOURCE: Page_7]

125   needed. This review may be helpful, for example, in defining a new research program in this [SOURCE: Page_7]

5 [SOURCE: Page_7]

126   climate region, which is highly sensitive to global warming and where snowmelt is an [SOURCE: Page_8]

127   important contributor to the hydrologic cycle. [SOURCE: Page_8]

128             More specifically this review seeks to answer the following questions: (1) what are [SOURCE: Page_8]

129   the major forcing variables and controls that drive the snow dynamics in Mediterranean [SOURCE: Page_8]

130   mountain regions? (2) To what extent are we able to estimate the spatiotemporal properties of [SOURCE: Page_8]

131   snowpack (i.e., snow cover areas (SCA), snow height (HS), and snow water equivalent [SOURCE: Page_8]

132   (SWE))? (3) What are the current limitations and opportunities associated with available [SOURCE: Page_8]

133   observational networks and methods used to assess the snowpack dynamics and hydrologic [SOURCE: Page_8]

134   responses in these mountain regions? [SOURCE: Page_8]

144             We collected 620 peer-reviewed articles published between 1913 and 2016, which [SOURCE: Page_8]

145   dealing with the snowpack and snow hydrology in Mediterranean regions. From this large [SOURCE: Page_8]

146   number of papers, we adopted a hybrid quantitative (metadata analysis from the papers) and [SOURCE: Page_8]

6 [SOURCE: Page_8]

147   qualitative approach (literature review) to gain insight into the major issues, trends, and [SOURCE: Page_9]

148   advances in this already large and growing research field. We present the geographic extent [SOURCE: Page_9]

149   of the papers and the methodology to analyze them in section (2). The key indicators obtained [SOURCE: Page_9]

150   from the papers dataset are presented in section (3). The second part of the paper is devoted [SOURCE: Page_9]

151   to the review of the three key issues that emerge from this article database: Section (4) [SOURCE: Page_9]

152   addresses the characterization of the climatic forcing in snow-dominated Mediterranean [SOURCE: Page_9]

153   regions; Section (5) describes the main features of the snowpack obtained from ground [SOURCE: Page_9]

154   measurements and remote sensing techniques; Section (6) focuses on the studies dedicated to [SOURCE: Page_9]

155   the snowmelt modeling; and Section (7) draws conclusions and opportunities for future work. [SOURCE: Page_9]

156          We focused on papers that address snowfall, snowpack and snow hydrology processes [SOURCE: Page_9]

157   in Mediterranean mountains. We did not focus on the climate change impact studies, which [SOURCE: Page_9]

158   already represent an important body of the literature per se. Interested readers can refer to [SOURCE: Page_9]

159   Beniston (2003), Beniston and Stoffel, (2014), de Jong et al., (eds) (2005), and de Jong et al., [SOURCE: Page_9]

160   (2012) for additional information regarding the potential impact of climate changes on [SOURCE: Page_9]

161   mountain hydrology and water resources in Mediterranean regions. [SOURCE: Page_9]

162 [SOURCE: Page_9]

163   2. Methods [SOURCE: Page_9]

164      2.1. Geographic extent of the review [SOURCE: Page_9]

165          The Mediterranean climate is found on the western part of the continents between [SOURCE: Page_9]

166   latitudes 30° and 45° (Bolle (ed.), 2003). The land regions around the Mediterranean Sea [SOURCE: Page_9]

167   (southern Europe, Northern Africa and the Levantine region) form the largest area of this [SOURCE: Page_9]

168   type of climate. The Mediterranean climate is also found in the western Coastal US [SOURCE: Page_9]

169   (California, and western Oregon), and the central Chilean Andes (Fig. 2). Limited regions in [SOURCE: Page_9]

170   southern Australia and southern Africa are also classified as Mediterranean (not shown). A [SOURCE: Page_9]

7 [SOURCE: Page_9]

171   combined Koppen (Kottek et al., 2006) and mountain classification scheme (Viviroli et al., [SOURCE: Page_10]

172   2007) was used to better distinguish the mountain regions associated with the Mediterranean [SOURCE: Page_10]

173   climate (Fig. 2). The Koppen climate scheme defines five main groups and multi subtypes [SOURCE: Page_10]

174   according to the long-time annual and monthly temperature means and precipitation totals. Of [SOURCE: Page_10]

175   particular interest to this study are the Mediterranean (Csa, Csb) regions and the regions [SOURCE: Page_10]

176   influenced by Mediterranean climate, such as the adjacent maritime zones (Cfb, Cfc) and the [SOURCE: Page_10]

177   semi-arid regions (BSh, BSk). The Viviroli et al., (2007) scheme distinguishes a total of 15 [SOURCE: Page_10]

178   relief patterns by combining elevation and a relief roughness indicator, and it was used to [SOURCE: Page_10]

179   distinguish between mountainous and lowland areas. [SOURCE: Page_10]

180 [SOURCE: Page_10]

181   Fig. 2. Mediterranean mountain regions (a) Central Chile (b) western US and (c) Mediterranean Basin. Mountain regions [SOURCE: Page_10]

182   were derived using the Viviroli et al., (2007) scheme based on the global multi-resolution terrain elevation data [SOURCE: Page_10]

183   (GMTED2010) at 15-arc second resolution (Danielson et al., 2011), and they are shown in light blue; the three main distinct [SOURCE: Page_10]

184   climates portray the Mediterranean (Csa, Csb) shown in light orange, oceanic and maritime climates (Cfb, Cfc) in dark olive, [SOURCE: Page_10]

185   and the semi-arid regions (BSh, BSk) in plum (Kottek et al., 2006). The red dot indicates the coordinates associated with [SOURCE: Page_10]

186   each paper in the database. [SOURCE: Page_10]

187 [SOURCE: Page_10]

188        2.2. Bibliographic sources [SOURCE: Page_10]

189             We used the website of the three major scientific publishers (Elsevier, Wiley, and [SOURCE: Page_10]

190   Springer), Google Scholar, ReadCube, and open access journals websites to find articles from [SOURCE: Page_10]

191   the early 20th century to the present, in which snow, hydrology, and mountain hydrology and [SOURCE: Page_10]

8 [SOURCE: Page_10]

192   climatology appeared in the title or abstract, or as a keyword. Only articles published in peer- [SOURCE: Page_11]

193   reviewed journal with an impact factor greater than one and articles related to the [SOURCE: Page_11]

194   Mediterranean regions presented above were considered. A second round of screening [SOURCE: Page_11]

195   focused on reading the abstract and identifying articles where snow properties, snow [SOURCE: Page_11]

196   hydrology, and mountain hydroclimatology are considered as part of the article’s main [SOURCE: Page_11]

197   objectives. As much as possible, we avoided papers where snow was addressed in a marginal [SOURCE: Page_11]

198   context. Hundreds of articles were identified during the initial screening. A total of 620 [SOURCE: Page_11]

199   articles, (published in 82 different journals) (Fig. 3a.), were retained after the second [SOURCE: Page_11]

200   screening. A table with the metadata for the articles is provided as supplementary file (A$_1$). [SOURCE: Page_11]

201          We identified a list of 35 different indicators that correspond to key research areas [SOURCE: Page_11]

202   (Supplementary material A$_2$). These were classified under three major groups (science, [SOURCE: Page_11]

203   methods, and data) and subdivided into different categories. The first group focuses on [SOURCE: Page_11]

204   science and it includes three categories: (1) meteorology and climatology in mountains (e.g., [SOURCE: Page_11]

205   mountain climate, climate change and variability, climatology and meteorology, and [SOURCE: Page_11]

206   hydrometeorology (inc. hydroclimatology), (2) snow (e.g., snow hydrometeorology (inc. [SOURCE: Page_11]

207   climatology), snow properties, and snow hydrology) and (3) hydrology (e.g., mountain [SOURCE: Page_11]

208   hydrology, hydrology in snow-dominated basins, and hydrogeology in mountains). The [SOURCE: Page_11]

209   second group focuses on the following methods: (4) methods used to quantify the snow cover [SOURCE: Page_11]

210   extent (e.g., remote sensing of snow); methods used to describe the spatial distribution of [SOURCE: Page_11]

211   snowpack indicators (e.g., spatial statistics and trends in climate, snowpack, and hydrology); [SOURCE: Page_11]

212   and methods used to simulate the main characteristics of snow accumulation, snow duration, [SOURCE: Page_11]

213   and snowmelt processes (e.g., snow energy and mass balance simulation, hydrological [SOURCE: Page_11]

214   modeling). The third group emphasizes the following: (5) data (i.e., length of data used, [SOURCE: Page_11]

215   source and type of data (i.e., ground observations, projections, and reanalysis), and spatial [SOURCE: Page_11]

216   scales (extent of the study area and elevation range). Each article was associated with a pair [SOURCE: Page_11]

9 [SOURCE: Page_11]

217   of longitude and latitude coordinates, which represent the approximate centroid of the study [SOURCE: Page_12]

218   area highlighted in the paper, and each article was reported on a map that shows both the [SOURCE: Page_12]

219   Koppen climate classes and the mountain regions, as defined by Viviroli et al., (2007) (Fig. [SOURCE: Page_12]

220   2). [SOURCE: Page_12]

221            Using our personal criteria, each article was classified under one or two major science [SOURCE: Page_12]

222   topics (see categories 1-3 above) and a methodological approach (see category 4 above). We [SOURCE: Page_12]

223   then combined both in a single label. For instance, Maurer et al. (2007) addressed streamflow [SOURCE: Page_12]

224   trends in snow-dominated regions, where climate change, hydrology, snow, and streamflow [SOURCE: Page_12]

225   timing were all considered. Hence, since the article emphasizes on the use of a hydrologic [SOURCE: Page_12]

226   model for streamflow in mountain regions, the paper was categorized under the “trends in [SOURCE: Page_12]

227   snowpack and hydrology, using distributed hydrological model”. [SOURCE: Page_12]

228 [SOURCE: Page_12]

229   3. Description of the articles database [SOURCE: Page_12]

230         3.1. Mountain ranges [SOURCE: Page_12]

231            Mountain regions covered in this review include the following (Fig. 3b): (1) the [SOURCE: Page_12]

232   coastal regions of western United States (Bales et al., 2006) including the Oregon Cascade [SOURCE: Page_12]

233   (Sproles et al., 2013) and California Sierra Nevada Mountains (Guan et al., 2013a; Molotch [SOURCE: Page_12]

234   and Meromy, 2014); (2) the central Andes that cover two of Chile’s major natural regions – [SOURCE: Page_12]

235   Chile’s central and southern zones (Favier et al., 2009; Cortés et al., 2011; Valdés-Pineda et [SOURCE: Page_12]

236   al., 2014); the major mountain chains around the Mediterranean Sea (López-Moreno et al., [SOURCE: Page_12]

237   2011a), namely; (3) Spanish (López-Moreno et al., 2014), and French (Gascoin et al., 2015), [SOURCE: Page_12]

238   Pyrenees; (4) Spanish Sierra Nevada Mountains (Pimentel et al. 2015); (5) French and Italian [SOURCE: Page_12]

239   Maritime Alps (Dedieu et al., 2014) – which are under the influence of the Mediterranean [SOURCE: Page_12]

240   climate (Durand et al. 2009); (6) Turkey and the Armenian Plateau (Tekeli, 2008); (7) Mount [SOURCE: Page_12]

10 [SOURCE: Page_12]

241   Lebanon and Anti Lebanon Ranges (Mhawej et al., 2014); and (8) Moroccan Atlas [SOURCE: Page_13]

242   (Marchane et al., 2015). Little information was found on other Mediterranean Sea mountains [SOURCE: Page_13]

243   (López-Moreno et al., 2011a), such as the Iberian Peninsula Mountains (Morán-Tejeda et al., [SOURCE: Page_13]

244   2014), Italian Apennines (Martelloni et al., 2013), Southern Calabrian mountains in Italy [SOURCE: Page_13]

245   (Senatore et al., 2011), Julian Alps (Italy and Slovenia), Dinaric Alps (mostly over Croatia [SOURCE: Page_13]

246   and Bosnia and Herzegovina), Bulgarian mountains (Brown and Petkova, 2007), Pindos [SOURCE: Page_13]

247   (Greece), Rhodope (Greece), and Taurus (Turkey). [SOURCE: Page_13]

248      3.2. Bibliometric analysis [SOURCE: Page_13]

249          Papers that covered local and regional scale studies accounted for ~90% (of all 620 [SOURCE: Page_13]

250   papers), the remaining (~10%) were global studies and review papers. The majority of all [SOURCE: Page_13]

251   articles (90.5%) were published between 2000 and 2016 inclusive, and half of all articles [SOURCE: Page_13]

252   (53.4%) were published over the past five years (2011-2016) (Fig. 3a). The remaining [SOURCE: Page_13]

253   (<10%, not shown) were published between 1913 and 1999, of which ~85% were published [SOURCE: Page_13]

254   between 1990 and 1999. Approximately 66.5% of all articles were published in 10 journals [SOURCE: Page_13]

255   (Fig. 3a). Over the past 16 years (2000-2016), papers covering the California, Sierra Nevada [SOURCE: Page_13]

256   Mountains amounted to 30% of the local and regional scale studies (41% when the regional [SOURCE: Page_13]

257   scale studies over the western United States are included). Spanish Pyrenees (10%) and the [SOURCE: Page_13]

258   central Chilean Andes (6%) were among the most studied mountain regions (Fig. 3b). Figure [SOURCE: Page_13]

259   3c highlights the yearly number of published papers in the first three major Mediterranean [SOURCE: Page_13]

260   regions (2000-2016). [SOURCE: Page_13]

269      3.3. Thematic analysis [SOURCE: Page_15]

270          The papers cover multiple scales: (1) experimental sites and small catchments (Storck [SOURCE: Page_15]

271   et al., 2002; Franz et al., 2010; López-Moreno et al., 2010, 2013b; Bales et al., 2011; Liu et [SOURCE: Page_15]

272   al., 2013; Raleigh et al., 2013; Harpold et al., 2014; Girotto et al., 2014; Revuelto et al., [SOURCE: Page_15]

273   2016a; Marti et al., 2016); (2) snow-dominated basins (Lundquist et al., 2010; Powell et al., [SOURCE: Page_15]

274   2011; Rousselot et al., 2012; Smith et al., 2013; Welch et al., 2013; Cortés et al., 2014; Franz [SOURCE: Page_15]

275   et al., 2014; Telesca et al., 2014; Marchane et al., 2015); (3) multiple basins, mountain [SOURCE: Page_15]

276   ranges, and regional scales (Painter et al., 2009; López-Moreno et al., 2011a; Pavelsky et al., [SOURCE: Page_15]

277   2011; Guan et al., 2013b; Núñez et al., 2013; Slater et al., 2013; Stewart, 2013; Avanzi et al., [SOURCE: Page_15]

278   2014; Hüsler et al., 2014; Cornwell et al., 2016); and (4) continental scales and the global [SOURCE: Page_15]

279   scale (e.g., Nohara et al., 2006; Pepin et al., 2015). The studies provide information on (1) [SOURCE: Page_15]

280   mountain climatology and meteorology (Bonfils et al., 2008; Herrero and Polo, 2012; [SOURCE: Page_15]

281   Pavelsky et al., 2012; Lute and Abatzoglou, 2014; Lundquist et al., 2015a; Guan et al., 2016); [SOURCE: Page_15]

282   (2) long-term hydroclimatology and snowpack trends in mountains (López-Moreno and [SOURCE: Page_15]

283   García-Ruiz, 2004; Hamlet et al., 2005; Mote et al., 2005; Stewart et al., 2005; Masiokas et [SOURCE: Page_15]

284   al., 2006; McCabe et al., 2007; Pierce et al., 2008; Das et al., 2009); (3) meteorological [SOURCE: Page_15]

285   forcing and topographic controls on snowfall and snowpack in mountains (Anderton et al., [SOURCE: Page_15]

286   2004; Guan et al., 2010; Rice et al., 2011; Musselman et al., 2012; Trujillo et al., 2012; [SOURCE: Page_15]

287   Wayand et al., 2013; Ayala et al., 2014; Molotch and Meromy, 2014; Hinkelman et al., 2015; [SOURCE: Page_15]

288   Lapo et al., 2015; López Moreno et al., 2015; Harpold, 2016); (4) snowpack properties [SOURCE: Page_15]

289   (Mizukami and Perica, 2008; Perrot et al., 2014; Trujillo and Molotch, 2014); (5) snow [SOURCE: Page_15]

290   accumulation and ablation (Harpold et al., 2012; Meromy et al., 2013; Guan et al., 2013a; [SOURCE: Page_15]

291   Avanzi et al., 2014; Sade et al., 2014); (6) snowmelt runoff (Tekeli et al., 2005a; Herrero et [SOURCE: Page_15]

292   al., 2009; orman et al., 2009; Franz and Karsten, 2013; Liu et al., 2013; Jepsen et al., [SOURCE: Page_15]

293   2016a); and (7) different hydrological (Aguilar et al., 2010; Kourgialas et al., 2010; [SOURCE: Page_15]

13 [SOURCE: Page_15]

294   Lundquist and Loheide, 2011; Goulden et al., 2012; Schlaepfer et al., 2012; Harpold et al., [SOURCE: Page_16]

295   2015; Penna et al., 2015; Harpold, 2016); and (8) hydrogeological processes (Tague and [SOURCE: Page_16]

296   Grant, 2009; Lowry et al., 2010) in snow-dominated mountain regions. [SOURCE: Page_16]

297          Given the variety of the topics covered in the literature, we found that the most [SOURCE: Page_16]

298   relevant approach for synthesizing them is to treat the three main vertical levels of the [SOURCE: Page_16]

299   hydrosystem from the atmosphere to the bedrock separately: (1) climate forcing to the [SOURCE: Page_16]

300   snowpack, (2) snowpack spatio-temporal variability, and (3) snowmelt hydrology and [SOURCE: Page_16]

301   hydrogeology. A synthesis on climate forcing, snowpack dynamics, and hydrological [SOURCE: Page_16]

302   processes is presented in Table 1 and further discussed in detail under sections 4-6, [SOURCE: Page_16]

303   respectively. Figure 4 summarizes the key elements of the energy fluxes and mass balance in [SOURCE: Page_16]

304   a typical Mediterranean mountain context. [SOURCE: Page_16]

305          Early studies published prior to the ‘90s emphasized on mountain meteorology, [SOURCE: Page_16]

306   ground observations of the snowpack, and the theoretical aspects of snow energy balance and [SOURCE: Page_16]

307   snowmelt-runoff models. The time period between 1990 and 1999 witnessed a diversification [SOURCE: Page_16]

308   in the topics with a focus on the quantification of the snow water equivalent, snow [SOURCE: Page_16]

309   interception in forests, hydrology and hydrogeology in snow-dominated basins –including [SOURCE: Page_16]

310   hydrochemical analysis of snowmelt runoff. In this period, the first studies on the changes in [SOURCE: Page_16]

311   snowpack and streamflow under global warming were published. Few studies were based on [SOURCE: Page_16]

312   remote sensing (5%). [SOURCE: Page_16]

313          After 2000, the number of scientific publications increased exponentially each five [SOURCE: Page_16]

314   years. Such a growth rate is higher than the doubling time for natural sciences, which is [SOURCE: Page_16]

315   estimated at 8.7 years (Bornmann and Mutz, 2015). Figure 5 summarizes the number of [SOURCE: Page_16]

316   published papers (2000-2016) based on their key category. The main scientific area was the [SOURCE: Page_16]

317   effect of meteorological and climatological variables on the snow hydrology that accounted [SOURCE: Page_16]

14 [SOURCE: Page_16]

318   for 29% of the studies (Fig. 5), of which about a half were dedicated to assessing climate [SOURCE: Page_17]

319   change impacts on the hydrology and the snowpack. 25% and 7% of the studies were [SOURCE: Page_17]

320   dedicated to hydrology and hydrogeology, respectively. The snowpack modeling accounted [SOURCE: Page_17]

321   for 13% of the studies, and the snow remote sensing accounted for 8% (Fig. 5). [SOURCE: Page_17]

361   4. Climate forcing to the snowpack [SOURCE: Page_19]

362            The two processes that control snowpack dynamics in mountains are snow [SOURCE: Page_19]

363   accumulation and ablation. The accumulation is mainly forced by snowfall and this mainly [SOURCE: Page_19]

364   depends on precipitation and temperature (López-Moreno, 2005), while the ablation is driven [SOURCE: Page_19]

365   by radiation and heat fluxes between the atmosphere, the snowpack and the underlying soil [SOURCE: Page_19]

366   (Herrero et al., 2009). The near-surface wind also influences both accumulation and ablation [SOURCE: Page_19]

367   (e.g., Gascoin et al., 2013) (see Sect. 5). [SOURCE: Page_19]
