368      4.1. Near-surface meteorological observations [SOURCE: Page_20]

369          The minimum meteorological data to run a temperature index snowmelt model are the [SOURCE: Page_20]

370   precipitation and the air temperature (DeWalle and Rango, 2008). A typical 1D (vertical) [SOURCE: Page_20]

371   snowpack energy balance model requires additional data of air relative humidity, incoming [SOURCE: Page_20]

372   shortwave and longwave radiation and wind speed. The wind direction is only useful to [SOURCE: Page_20]

373   simulate wind drift and lateral heat advection (e.g., Burns et al., 2014), in case of a patchy [SOURCE: Page_20]

374   snow cover (e.g., Liston, 1995). However, information on the spatial distribution of the main [SOURCE: Page_20]

375   meteorological variables remains incomplete in Mediterranean mountains, such as in most of [SOURCE: Page_20]

376   the world’s mountains (e.g., Bales et al., 2006). This has been attributed to the following: [SOURCE: Page_20]

377          (1) The sparseness of the automatic weather stations (AWS) networks (Horel and [SOURCE: Page_20]

378   Dong, 2010; Gottardi et al., 2012; Valdés-Pineda et al., 2014; Henn et al., 2015). Even in [SOURCE: Page_20]

379   well-monitored regions, most of the AWS are usually below 3000 m a.s.l, although meltwater [SOURCE: Page_20]

380   production generally increases with elevation (Favier et al., 2009). [SOURCE: Page_20]

381          (2) The number of variables being measured at the AWS (Raleigh et al., 2016). A [SOURCE: Page_20]

382   survey based on 1318 AWS across the western US, where meteorological data and either [SOURCE: Page_20]

383   SWE or snow depth are measured, indicated that near-surface forcing variables of air [SOURCE: Page_20]

384   temperature and precipitation are among the most sampled (99-83%), whereas wind speed, [SOURCE: Page_20]

385   humidity (often as relative humidity RH), and incoming shortwave radiation are measured [SOURCE: Page_20]

386   24-36% of the time. Incoming longwave radiation is the least measured (1.4%). Nearly 99% [SOURCE: Page_20]

387   of all AWS do not measure all six forcing variables (Raleigh et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_20]

388          (3) Biases and uncertainties that arise from observational data. AWS are prone to data [SOURCE: Page_20]

389   logging failure; some sensors require periodic calibration and the data must undergo specific [SOURCE: Page_20]

390   quality control given the harsh climatic conditions encountered in high-elevation areas [SOURCE: Page_20]

391   (Estévez et al., 2011; Filippa et al., 2014; Lundquist et al., 2015a). [SOURCE: Page_20]

18 [SOURCE: Page_20]

392            Radiative and turbulent fluxes in Mediterranean mountain regions can be calculated [SOURCE: Page_21]

393   from meteorological and incident solar radiation and thermal radiation observations (e.g., [SOURCE: Page_21]

394   using pyranometers and pyrgeometers) at experimental sites (e.g., Marks et al., 1992). There [SOURCE: Page_21]

395   are few flux tower locations (e.g., Goulden et al., 2012; Burns et al, 2014) and specific [SOURCE: Page_21]

396   research sites, such as the CUES in California, which feature a full range of censors for [SOURCE: Page_21]

397   measuring the snow energy balance (Bair et al., 2015). Flux towers equipped with eddy [SOURCE: Page_21]

398   covariance system and specific stations equipped with pyranometers and pyrgeometers [SOURCE: Page_21]

399   remain the two options for measuring the sensible heat, latent heat, and radiative fluxes. [SOURCE: Page_21]

400   However, most of the available flux towers are generally located at flatter lowland areas and [SOURCE: Page_21]

401   provide little information needed to evaluate fluxes in snow ablation in complex mountain [SOURCE: Page_21]

402   regions (Bales et al., 2006; Raleigh et al., 2016). Studies that address snow energy balance in [SOURCE: Page_21]

403   snow-dominated regions (e.g., Sproles et al., 2013; Mernild et al., 2016a) rely on solving the [SOURCE: Page_21]

404   energy balance solely from meteorological observations (e.g., Liston et al., 1999; Herrero et [SOURCE: Page_21]

405   al., 2009). This has resulted in the current availability of limited information for validating [SOURCE: Page_21]

406   the contribution of radiative and turbulent fluxes (Lapo et al., 2015; Raleigh et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_21]

407      4.2. Precipitation [SOURCE: Page_21]

408            In Mediterranean mountain regions, more than 80% of the annual precipitation falls [SOURCE: Page_21]

409   during winter (November to March for the Northern Hemisphere and May to September in [SOURCE: Page_21]

410   the Southern Hemisphere) (Mazurkiewicz et al., 2008; Demaria et al. 2013a; López-Moreno [SOURCE: Page_21]

411   et al. 2013a). Mountain regions such as the Sierra Nevada, California receive 75-90% of [SOURCE: Page_21]

412   winter precipitation in the form of snow (Jepsen et al., 2012). Mountain regions tend to [SOURCE: Page_21]

413   receive more precipitation than surrounding lowland areas due to the orographic [SOURCE: Page_21]

414   enhancement (Favier et al., 2009; Neiman et al., 2014; Lundquist et al., 2015a; Derin et al., [SOURCE: Page_21]

415   2016). [SOURCE: Page_21]

19 [SOURCE: Page_21]

416          The assessment of the distribution in precipitation amounts in mountain regions and [SOURCE: Page_22]

417   an accurate identification of the precipitation type (snow or rain) emerge as the most [SOURCE: Page_22]

418   uncertain factors in the literature (Gottardi et al., 2012; Neiman et al., 2014; Dettinger, 2014; [SOURCE: Page_22]

419   Buisan et al., 2014; Derin et al., 2016). Uncertainties in the precipitation distribution over [SOURCE: Page_22]

420   mountain regions are associated with: (1) snow gauge undercatch (Sevruk et al, 1991; [SOURCE: Page_22]

421   Rasmussen et al., 2012), (2) precipitation phase determination (e.g., Harpold et al., 2017), (3) [SOURCE: Page_22]

422   methods for estimating spatial precipitation distribution, including the precipitation lapse [SOURCE: Page_22]

423   rates (Lundquist et al., 2015a; Henn et al., 2017), and (4) the heterogeneity of the [SOURCE: Page_22]

424   precipitation network (Favier et al., 2009; Rice and Bales, 2010; and Gottardi et al., 2012). [SOURCE: Page_22]

425   Wind-induced precipitation undercatch affects the accuracy of precipitation data and is more [SOURCE: Page_22]

426   pronounced for solid precipitation than for liquid precipitation (Rasmussen et al., 2012; [SOURCE: Page_22]

427   Buisan et al., 2016; Smith et al., 2016; Pan et al., 2016). Measurement errors due to gauge [SOURCE: Page_22]

428   undercatch frequently range between 20% and 50% (Rasmussen et al., 2012). The factors that [SOURCE: Page_22]

429   govern snow undercatch are related to the gauge setting (i.e. shielded and unshielded) (Colli [SOURCE: Page_22]

430   et al., 2015), snowflake characteristics (Theriault et al., 2012), and wind speed (Rasmussen et [SOURCE: Page_22]

431   al., 2012). Despite its importance little information was found in the literature on snow [SOURCE: Page_22]

432   undercatch in Mediterranean regions. [SOURCE: Page_22]

433          Precipitation phase determination is often done as post-processing given that most [SOURCE: Page_22]

434   gauges do not detect the phase. The precipitation phase is required to determine the water [SOURCE: Page_22]

435   equivalent during the accumulation season, but also to properly correct precipitation [SOURCE: Page_22]

436   undercatch, and for investigating rain-on-snow events which contribute to the snowpack [SOURCE: Page_22]

437   ablation. Determining precipitation phase from ground observation can be achieved using: (1) [SOURCE: Page_22]

438   thresholds for temperature (e.g., air, dewpoint, and wet bulb) (see Marks et al., 2013), (2) [SOURCE: Page_22]

439   linear transition, range, and sigmoidal curve methods based on near-surface air temperature [SOURCE: Page_22]

440   (e.g., Harpold et al., 2017), and (3) psychrometric energy balance method (Harder and [SOURCE: Page_22]

20 [SOURCE: Page_22]

441   Pomeroy, 2013). Despite the availability of these methods, one of the major limitation for the [SOURCE: Page_23]

442   proper determination of precipitation phase from ground data is attributed to the lack of [SOURCE: Page_23]

443   meteorological observations for stations in near locations to the precipitation gauges (Harpold [SOURCE: Page_23]

444   et al., 2017). [SOURCE: Page_23]

445           Rain-on-snow events are not uncommon in Mediterranean mountains (Guan et al., [SOURCE: Page_23]

446   2016). These events help in warming the snowpack and add very high sensible heat to the [SOURCE: Page_23]

447   snowpack through condensation, which accelerates melting and sometimes triggers [SOURCE: Page_23]

448   catastrophic floods (McCabe et al., 2007; Surfleet and Tullos, 2013). In the western US, rain- [SOURCE: Page_23]

449   on-snow-events appear to be partly driven by the El Niño–Southern Oscillation (ENSO), the [SOURCE: Page_23]

450   Pacific Decadal Oscillation (PDO) (McCabe et al., 2007; Lute and Abatzoglou, 2014) and [SOURCE: Page_23]

451   atmospheric rivers (Trujillo and Molotch, 2014; Guan et al., 2016). To our knowledge, the [SOURCE: Page_23]

452   impacts of rain-on-snow events were not specifically addressed in regions other than the [SOURCE: Page_23]

453   western US. The analysis of rain-on-snow events remain challenging and warrant future [SOURCE: Page_23]

454   investigation (Wayand et al., 2015). [SOURCE: Page_23]

455           Uncertainties associated with the spatial distribution of precipitation increases with [SOURCE: Page_23]

456   elevation, where differences between datasets ranged between 5 to 60%, exceeding 200 mm [SOURCE: Page_23]

457   yr-1 on average, were reported across the western US (Henn et al., 2017). Different [SOURCE: Page_23]

458   methodologies were developed to compute the integrated precipitation in high-elevation [SOURCE: Page_23]

459   watersheds (Valery et al., 2010) and to generate gridded precipitation maps from daily [SOURCE: Page_23]

460   ground observation (see Demaria et al., (2013b) and Lundquist et al., (2015a) for a list of [SOURCE: Page_23]

461   gridded precipitation products). The emergence of gridded precipitation products over the [SOURCE: Page_23]

462   2000s has boosted the research in snow hydrology and facilitated the upscaling of point-scale [SOURCE: Page_23]

463   studies. Precipitation grids are invaluable data for distributed hydrological modeling in both [SOURCE: Page_23]

464   the operational and research context, e.g., to perform climate change impact studies at the [SOURCE: Page_23]

465   scale of river basins (Mernild et al., 2016b). While the overall accuracy of gridded [SOURCE: Page_23]

21 [SOURCE: Page_23]

466   precipitation products has been deemed satisfactory, the biases increase with elevation [SOURCE: Page_24]

467   (Mizukami et al., 2011; Lundquist et al., 2015a). Precipitation-gauge undercatch, a poor [SOURCE: Page_24]

468   knowledge of wind patterns to correct the undercatch, and the lack of in situ input data are [SOURCE: Page_24]

469   sources of errors in the gridded precipitation products (Dettinger, 2014; Lundquist et al., [SOURCE: Page_24]

470   2015b; Mizukami and Smith, 2012). In addition, temporal inconsistencies in multi-year [SOURCE: Page_24]

471   gridded precipitation biases tend to increase the uncertainty in simulating hydrological [SOURCE: Page_24]

472   responses in snow-dominated basins (Mizukami and Smith, 2012). [SOURCE: Page_24]

473          Acquiring precipitation and snowfall from high-resolution satellite-based rainfall [SOURCE: Page_24]

474   (SBR) products (e.g., TRMM and GPM) in mountain regions (Derin et al., 2016), such as the [SOURCE: Page_24]

475   western US (Behrangi et al., 2016), and northeastern Spain (Kenawy et al., 2015), is still [SOURCE: Page_24]

476   hindered by the facts that (1) capturing solid precipitation is practically challenging for [SOURCE: Page_24]

477   TRMM and results are highly biased over snow surfaces, (2) precipitation satellites tend to [SOURCE: Page_24]

478   underestimate the wet season and overestimate dry season precipitation rates over land and [SOURCE: Page_24]

479   should be corrected using in situ measurements, and (3) the performance of gauge correction [SOURCE: Page_24]

480   to SRB is less effective in mountain regions and depends on the representativeness of the [SOURCE: Page_24]

481   observation networks. [SOURCE: Page_24]

482          Over the past decade there had been an increase in the use of assimilation techniques [SOURCE: Page_24]

483   and the downscaling of atmospheric model (e.g., Vionnet et al., 2016, Mernild et al., 2016a). [SOURCE: Page_24]

484   These different techniques to estimate precipitation using a combination of ground [SOURCE: Page_24]

485   observations, numerical weather prediction, and remote sensing observations are still not [SOURCE: Page_24]

486   complete and the generated gridded data is at coarser and medium resolution at best (e.g., [SOURCE: Page_24]

487   Quéno et al., 2016). There is more work needed to understand how atmospheric circulation [SOURCE: Page_24]

488   (Jin et al., 2006; Lundquist et al., 2010) and the complex topography at the finer scales [SOURCE: Page_24]

489   (López-Moreno et al., 2015) affects precipitation and snowfall and both topics are interesting [SOURCE: Page_24]

490   field of research. An intercomparison between different gridded precipitation datasets (e.g., [SOURCE: Page_24]

22 [SOURCE: Page_24]

491   Lundquist et al., 2015a; Henn et al., 2017) across different mountain regions is needed. [SOURCE: Page_25]

492   Reducing the bias in precipitation estimates along altitude and enhancing the spatial [SOURCE: Page_25]

493   resolution of gridded precipitation could be the next breakthrough in mountain hydrology. [SOURCE: Page_25]

494      4.3. Air temperature [SOURCE: Page_25]

495          The air temperature is the most important factor that determines the precipitation [SOURCE: Page_25]

496   phase (rain or snow), and it is strongly correlated to the snowmelt rate (Jin et al., 2006; Mote, [SOURCE: Page_25]

497   2006; Brown and Petkova, 2007; Kapnick and Hall, 2010). During the melting season in the [SOURCE: Page_25]

498   mid-elevation regions of the Spanish Pyrenes, the increase in the daily snowmelt rates was [SOURCE: Page_25]

499   correlated with the increase in the observed daily temperature (López Moreno and Latron, [SOURCE: Page_25]

500   2008). In many low and mid-elevation Mediterranean mountain regions, changes in the [SOURCE: Page_25]

501   surface air temperature had been previously identified as the major driver for the reduction in [SOURCE: Page_25]

502   the amount of precipitation that falls as snow, the decline in the snow water equivalent, and [SOURCE: Page_25]

503   shifts towards earlier snowmelt (Stewart et al., 2005; Bonfils et al., 2008; Demaria et al. [SOURCE: Page_25]

504   2013a; Pagán et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_25]

505          Similar, to all other meteorological variables, uncertainties associated with measuring [SOURCE: Page_25]

506   the air temperature arise from the lack of AWS in remote and high-elevation mountain areas [SOURCE: Page_25]

507   (Raleigh et al., 2016). However, air temperature is the climatic variable that is the most often [SOURCE: Page_25]

508   available from in situ and gridded-products. In comparison with precipitation, the in-situ [SOURCE: Page_25]

509   measurements of temperature are much less biased and the interpolation methods are more [SOURCE: Page_25]

510   robust. From the papers we could examine, the uncertainty on the precipitation data is [SOURCE: Page_25]

511   generally considered as a more important issue. [SOURCE: Page_25]

512      4.4. Radiation and heat fluxes [SOURCE: Page_25]

513          In Mediterranean mountain regions, the snowpack melt energy is dominated by the [SOURCE: Page_25]

514   radiative fluxes (shortwave and longwave) (Marks and Dozier, 1992; López Moreno et al., [SOURCE: Page_25]

23 [SOURCE: Page_25]

515   2012). The net radiative flux accounts for approximately 70-80% of the energy for snow melt [SOURCE: Page_26]

516   in regions, such as Sierra Nevada California, Oregon Cascades, Spanish Pyrenees, and the [SOURCE: Page_26]

517   Armenian Plateau (Marks and Dozier, 1992; ensoy et al., 2006; Mazurkiewicz et al., 2008; [SOURCE: Page_26]

518   López-Moreno et al., 2008b; Hinkelman et al., 2015). However, this contribution varies [SOURCE: Page_26]

519   significantly over the time and the location. For instance, the net radiation contribution to [SOURCE: Page_26]

520   melt at three different sites in the Oregon cascades was found to range between 49 and 80% [SOURCE: Page_26]

521   (Mazurkiewicz et al., 2008). Similar results were found in the Armenian Plateau, which [SOURCE: Page_26]

522   indicates that the net radiation fluxes and turbulent fluxes account for 70 to 30% of the melt [SOURCE: Page_26]

523   energy, respectively ( ensoy et al., 2006). Sensible and latent heat transfers were found to be [SOURCE: Page_26]

524   of similar magnitude with an opposite sign, and therefore they tend to cancel each other in [SOURCE: Page_26]

525   some sites during the now-melt season (Marks and Dozier, 1992; Jepsen et al., 2012). A [SOURCE: Page_26]

526   significant amount of snow is lost through sublimation in Mediterranean mountains, [SOURCE: Page_26]

527   approximately 20% of the total snowpack ablation depending on the location, and this [SOURCE: Page_26]

528   amount increases at the end of the snow season (Beaty, 1975; Marks and Dozier, 1992; [SOURCE: Page_26]

529   Jepsen et al., 2012). The net turbulent flux tends to increase during the melt season. Its [SOURCE: Page_26]

530   contribution to snowmelt during the entire season was found to range between 0 to 19% in a [SOURCE: Page_26]

531   mountain basin in Sierra Nevada, California (a mean of 10% with a standard deviation of 6% [SOURCE: Page_26]

532   over a time period of 12 years) (Jepsen et al., 2012). The contribution of the turbulent energy [SOURCE: Page_26]

533   fluxes was found to increase under rain-on-snow events, and it accounts for up to 42% of [SOURCE: Page_26]

534   snowmelt during such events (Mazurkiewicz et al., 2008). The contribution of turbulent [SOURCE: Page_26]

535   fluxes (i.e., latent and sensible heat fluxes) to melt energy was found to be slightly higher in [SOURCE: Page_26]

536   warmer    Mediterranean regions (Atlas Mountain,           Iberian Peninsula      and   Eastern [SOURCE: Page_26]

537   Mediterranean) (Schulz and de Jong, 2004; Herrero et al., 2009; Sade et al., 2014). Under [SOURCE: Page_26]

538   extreme weather conditions (i.e., episodic strong low-humidity winds, clear skies, intense [SOURCE: Page_26]

539   solar radiation, and sudden increases in temperatures), sublimation accounted for up to 40% [SOURCE: Page_26]

24 [SOURCE: Page_26]

540   of the total loss in snowpack in Sierra Nevada, Spain (Herrero et al., 2009), and similar [SOURCE: Page_27]

541   results were reported for the Atlas Mountain (Schulz and de Jong, 2004) and Mount Hermon [SOURCE: Page_27]

542   (Sade et al., 2014). [SOURCE: Page_27]

543            In the absence of radiation sensors (pyranometer and pyrgeometer), the incoming [SOURCE: Page_27]

544   radiation can be estimated using (1) empirical relationships with surface air temperature and [SOURCE: Page_27]

545   relative humidity (Jepsen et al., 2012; Lundquist et al., 2013) or (2) solar and longwave [SOURCE: Page_27]

546   surface irradiance data from synoptic satellite products (Jepsen et al., 2012; Hinkelman et al., [SOURCE: Page_27]

547   2015). However, computing radiative fluxes using empirical methods can lead to [SOURCE: Page_27]

548   overestimation errors of up to 50% in the snowmelt rates, which indicates the need for [SOURCE: Page_27]

549   enhanced methods that combines satellite products and ground observations (e.g., Hinkelman [SOURCE: Page_27]

550   et al., 2015). The application of such products to Mediterranean mountains should account for [SOURCE: Page_27]

551   the fact that shortwave and longwave radiations vary significantly with elevation, terrain [SOURCE: Page_27]

552   geometry and the forest cover (Aguilar et al., 2010; Raleigh et al., 2013, Lundquist et al., [SOURCE: Page_27]

553   2013). [SOURCE: Page_27]

554            Sensible and latent heat exchange are influenced by surface air temperature, water [SOURCE: Page_27]

555   vapor, and wind speed (Marks and Dozier, 1992; Kim and Kang, 2007; Jepsen et al., 2012). [SOURCE: Page_27]

556   Most of these variables exhibit high spatial variability over the complex mountainous [SOURCE: Page_27]

557   topography (Marks and Dozier, 1992), which makes it difficult to achieve reliable estimates [SOURCE: Page_27]

558   of the contribution of snowmelt from heat fluxes at most sites. Furthermore, while the surface [SOURCE: Page_27]

559   air temperature is measured at most AWS, information on wind speed and humidity are often [SOURCE: Page_27]

560   lacking, and thus, this hinders the calculation of sensible heat fluxes (Raleigh et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_27]

561            Our understanding of the energy exchange between the snowpack and the atmosphere [SOURCE: Page_27]

562   in mountains is incomplete. The installation of more complete meteorological stations [SOURCE: Page_27]

563   (including longwave/shortwave sensors and turbulent heat measurement systems) in [SOURCE: Page_27]

25 [SOURCE: Page_27]

564   mountains is needed in order to better characterize the different components of the energy [SOURCE: Page_28]

565   balance. However, this requires solving the issue that eddy covariance systems are not [SOURCE: Page_28]

566   considered reliable in complex terrain. [SOURCE: Page_28]

567   5. Snowpack [SOURCE: Page_28]

568      5.1. In situ observations [SOURCE: Page_28]

569          Historical studies on snow properties and the quantification of the water that falls as [SOURCE: Page_28]

570   snow are more than 300 years old (Grew, 1673), and the importance of snow cover on [SOURCE: Page_28]

571   climate was first highlighted by Woeikof (1885). The first published investigations of [SOURCE: Page_28]

572   snowfall, HS, snow density, SWE and snow melt, and the first method for field sampling of [SOURCE: Page_28]

573   snow and snow instrumentation in California can be dated to the late 19th and early 20th [SOURCE: Page_28]

574   centuries (e.g., Church, 1913). Monthly snowfall observations in California date back to 1878 [SOURCE: Page_28]

575   (Christy, 2012). Over the past two decades, there has been a significant increase in the [SOURCE: Page_28]

576   number of studies that investigates HS, snow density, and SWE through manual field [SOURCE: Page_28]

577   sampling (Bocchiola and Groppelli, 2010; Sturm et al., 2010; López-Moreno et al., 2013b; [SOURCE: Page_28]

578   Bormann et al., 2013; Ayala et al., 2014), HS measurements using (acoustic) snow depth [SOURCE: Page_28]

579   gauges, SWE measurements with snow pillows, and snow density measurements from HS [SOURCE: Page_28]

580   and SWE observations (e.g., SNOTEL network) (e.g., Rice and Bales, 2010; Meromy et al., [SOURCE: Page_28]

581   2013; Luce et al., 2014). [SOURCE: Page_28]

582          The main variable of interest for snow hydrologists is the SWE, which is obtained by [SOURCE: Page_28]

583   multiplying the snow height by the snow density. The snow height is typically measured with [SOURCE: Page_28]

584   a calibrated snow probe and it is easier to estimate than the snow density, which requires a [SOURCE: Page_28]

585   more elaborate field-work (e.g., Bocchiola and Groppeli, 2010; Sturm et al., 2010; López- [SOURCE: Page_28]

586   Moreno et al., 2011b). Field measurements are usually biased with observational errors [SOURCE: Page_28]

587   (Lundquist et al., 2015b) and uncertainty about the representativeness of in-situ snow depth [SOURCE: Page_28]

26 [SOURCE: Page_28]

588   and density measurements (López-Moreno et al., 2011b, 2013b). Improving the in-situ [SOURCE: Page_29]

589   accuracy of snow course measurements can be achieved by increasing the rate of data [SOURCE: Page_29]

590   sampling along the snow course and minimizing human errors. Findings in the Spanish [SOURCE: Page_29]

591   Pyrenees indicated that increasing the number of in-situ measurements (i.e., using at least five [SOURCE: Page_29]

592   snow depth measurements at 5-10 meter intervals) could ensure a bias of less than 10% when [SOURCE: Page_29]

593   estimating the average snow depth at plots of 100 m^2 (López-Moreno et al., 2011b). The [SOURCE: Page_29]

594   snow density exhibits less variability than the snow depth (López-Moreno et al., 2013b), [SOURCE: Page_29]

595   which implies the need for fewer snow density measurements along each course. Such [SOURCE: Page_29]

596   findings support the US snow courses sampling guidelines, which indicates the need for 10 [SOURCE: Page_29]

597   point measurements of HS, SWE, and density at 30 meter interval (over a 300 m transect) [SOURCE: Page_29]

598   (Rice and Bales, 2010). The same applies for snow sampling approaches in other regions [SOURCE: Page_29]

599   (e.g., Watson et al., 2006; Jost et al., 2007). However, collecting in-situ probe measurements [SOURCE: Page_29]

600   at larger scales remains challenging. Steeps slopes and high-elevation areas (above 3000 m [SOURCE: Page_29]

601   a.s.l) are unsafe and not easily accessible. Airborne and terrestrial Lidar now enable the [SOURCE: Page_29]

602   measurement of HS with a centimetric accuracy at a much higher resolution than manual [SOURCE: Page_29]

603   surveys (Sect. 5.2.4). However, these techniques remain costly and do not provide continuous [SOURCE: Page_29]

604   observation of HS, such as AWS. A terrestrial time-lapse camera is a cost-effective device [SOURCE: Page_29]

605   used to monitor the snow cover variability in both space and time, but its use is restricted to [SOURCE: Page_29]

606   small spatial scales (less than 1 km²) (Revuelto et al., 2014). As a result, it is difficult to [SOURCE: Page_29]

607   capture the temporal variability of the HS and SWE by field sampling. On the other hand, [SOURCE: Page_29]

608   continuous AWS measurements of snow depth and SWE remain site specific, and most of the [SOURCE: Page_29]

609   time, they fail to capture the spatial variability of the snowpack due to the spatial [SOURCE: Page_29]

610   heterogeneity of the climate and terrain with respect to the network density (Bales et al., [SOURCE: Page_29]

611   2006; Gottardi et al., 2012; Raleigh et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_29]

27 [SOURCE: Page_29]

612          Some regions are equipped with advanced snow observatories and the data are easily [SOURCE: Page_30]

613   accessible (e.g., SNOTEL in the western US) while other mountain regions, such as the [SOURCE: Page_30]

614   Pyrenees, are well covered by ground stations but the data are to be collected from various [SOURCE: Page_30]

615   agencies and are not always publicly distributed (e.g., Gascoin et al., 2015). The [SOURCE: Page_30]

616   implementation of snow observatories based on the principles of open data is en route in [SOURCE: Page_30]

617   regions such as Lebanon where snow observations are recently being collected (e.g., Fayad et [SOURCE: Page_30]

618   al., 2017). Extending the ground-based observation networks remains crucial in mountain [SOURCE: Page_30]

619   regions that remain under-sampled. However, it is as important to share these data to foster [SOURCE: Page_30]

620   their use by the scientific community and among water stakeholders. [SOURCE: Page_30]

621      5.2. Remote sensing of seasonal snow cover [SOURCE: Page_30]

622          The snow cover extent, albedo, height, and water equivalent are the main remote [SOURCE: Page_30]

623   sensing products for snow hydrology, but they have very different levels of accuracy and [SOURCE: Page_30]

624   resolution. Here, we only briefly present the main products that were used in our list of [SOURCE: Page_30]

625   papers. Interested readers can refer to Dietz et al. (2012) and Frei et al. (2012) for further [SOURCE: Page_30]

626   information on snow remote sensing. [SOURCE: Page_30]

627          5.2.1. Optical remote sensing of the snow cover [SOURCE: Page_30]

628          The NASA MODIS Aqua/Terra daily snow products (collection 5 MOD10A$_1$ and [SOURCE: Page_30]

629   MYD10A$_1$ (Hall et al., 2002)) are the most widely used. These products have provided the [SOURCE: Page_30]

630   binary snow-covered area (SCA) and fractional SCA (fSCA), and the snow albedo at a 500 m [SOURCE: Page_30]

631   resolution since 2000 for Terra and 2002 for Aqua. The SCA product allows the calculation [SOURCE: Page_30]

632   of the snow cover duration (SCD), snow cover start date (SCS) and snow cover melt-out date [SOURCE: Page_30]

633   (SCM). The SCA and fSCA allow the calculation of the snow coverage in a watershed. [SOURCE: Page_30]

634   MODIS snow products were tested and applied in the Sierra Nevada California (Painter et al., [SOURCE: Page_30]

635   2009; Molotch and Meromy, 2014), Pyrenees (Gascoin et al., 2015), Southern Alps (Dedieu [SOURCE: Page_30]

28 [SOURCE: Page_30]

636   et al., 2014), Moroccan Atlas (Marchane et al., 2015), Armenian Plateau (Tekeli et al., 2006), [SOURCE: Page_31]

637   and Mount Lebanon (Telesca et al., 2014). [SOURCE: Page_31]

638          The main limitation of optical snow products is the obstruction by cloud cover. A [SOURCE: Page_31]

639   cloud removal algorithm must be run to generate meaningful snow climatology from MODIS [SOURCE: Page_31]

640   snow products (Gascoin et al., 2015; Marchane et al., 2015). There are also errors related to [SOURCE: Page_31]

641   sensor viewing geometry and forest canopy obstruction (Dozier et al., 2008; Gao et al., 2010; [SOURCE: Page_31]

642   Dietz et al. 2012; Raleigh et al., 2013; Kostadinov and Lookingbill, 2015). [SOURCE: Page_31]

643          In addition to the MOD10 family, there are more sophisticated approaches to retrieve [SOURCE: Page_31]

644   sub-pixel fSCA, grain size, and albedo from MODIS using spectral unmixing techniques [SOURCE: Page_31]

645   (Painter et al., 2009). The validation of the fSCA against higher resolution snow cover maps [SOURCE: Page_31]

646   data obtained from Landsat ETM+ over the Sierra Nevada California, in particular, indicates [SOURCE: Page_31]

647   their better accuracy compared to the MOD10A$_1$ products (Dozier et al., 2009; Painter et al., [SOURCE: Page_31]

648   2009; Rittger et al., 2013). The accuracy of the fSCA is lower in forested areas, which [SOURCE: Page_31]

649   suggests that there is a need for future research in this direction (Raleigh et al., 2013; [SOURCE: Page_31]

650   Kostadinov and Lookingbill, 2015). [SOURCE: Page_31]

651          Snow cover extent and snow climatology had also been derived from AVHRR data at [SOURCE: Page_31]

652   a spatial resolution of 1 km for the Alpine region (1985–2011) (Hüsler et al., 2014) and the [SOURCE: Page_31]

653   Armenian Plateau (Tekeli et al., 2005a). Finally, Cortés et al., (2014) proposed and tested a [SOURCE: Page_31]

654   sub-pixel approach for mapping snow and ice cover over the central Andes using spectral [SOURCE: Page_31]

655   unmixing of Landsat imagery at a spatial resolution of 30 m (1986–2013). [SOURCE: Page_31]

656          Future improvement of snow cover representativeness from optical remote sensing [SOURCE: Page_31]

657   could include better cloud removal and enhanced snow cover mapping in forested regions. [SOURCE: Page_31]

658   Daily SCA from MODIS are hindered by their spatial resolution of 500m which is too coarse [SOURCE: Page_31]

659   to capture snow variability in heterogenous mountain slopes, in regions where snow is [SOURCE: Page_31]

660   ephemeral, and at the end of the season when snow becomes patchy. The Sentinel-2 mission [SOURCE: Page_31]

29 [SOURCE: Page_31]

661   with its 20 m spatial resolution and 5 days revisit time is expected to provide better accuracy [SOURCE: Page_32]

662   for mapping SCA in the heterogenous mountain regions. [SOURCE: Page_32]

663            5.2.2. Remote sensing of SWE [SOURCE: Page_32]

664            The retrieval of SWE form spaceborne passive microwave (PM) sensors involves a [SOURCE: Page_32]

665   coarser resolution (~25 km) and limited accuracy (Dietz et al., 2012; Frei et al., 2012; [SOURCE: Page_32]

666   Vuyovich et al., 2014; Dozier et al., 2016). Overall, PM sensors tend to underestimate SWE. [SOURCE: Page_32]

667   For example, from April 1st 2014 SWE estimates, the passive microwave AMSR$_2$ sensor [SOURCE: Page_32]

668   underestimated the SWE by 40-75% compared to other SWE retrieval methods (i.e., [SOURCE: Page_32]

669   interpolation from snow pillows and SCA, calculation using SCA and NLDAS, and modeling [SOURCE: Page_32]

670   using SNODAS) (Dozier et al., 2016). Examples from the Mediterranean regions include the [SOURCE: Page_32]

671   use of AMSRE/AMSR$_2$ over the contiguous US (Vuyovich et al., 2014), California (Li et al., [SOURCE: Page_32]

672   2012), the Armenian Plateau (Tekeli, 2008; orman and Beser, 2013), and Mount Lebanon [SOURCE: Page_32]

673   (Mhawej et al., 2014), and the use of the Scanning Multichannel Microwave Radiometer [SOURCE: Page_32]

674   (SMMR) and Special Sensor Microwave Imagers (SSM/I) over the Andes (Foster et al., [SOURCE: Page_32]

675   2009). [SOURCE: Page_32]

676            PM sensors have limited capability in capturing the SWE in regions with ephemeral [SOURCE: Page_32]

677   or patchy snow cover and regions with vegetation cover (Li et al., 2012; Vuyovich et al., [SOURCE: Page_32]

678   2014), and they exhibit an overall tendency to underestimate SWE during snowfall and melt [SOURCE: Page_32]

679   seasons (Dozier et al., 2016). This was attributed to the high scattering in mountain regions [SOURCE: Page_32]

680   due to relief (Li et al., 2012) and the tendency of PMs to saturate at an SWE value of 120 mm [SOURCE: Page_32]

681   (for wavelengths, 18 GHz as the background signal and 37 GHz as the scattering signal) [SOURCE: Page_32]

682   (Dietz et al. 2012). [SOURCE: Page_32]

683            Despite these limitations of PMs, a number of studies demonstrated that PMs are able [SOURCE: Page_32]

684   to capture the overall seasonal variations of snow accumulation, melt timing, and season [SOURCE: Page_32]

685   length at the macro scale, and in contrast to imaging spectrometry, they are not affected by [SOURCE: Page_32]

30 [SOURCE: Page_32]

686   cloud cover (Foster et al., 2009; Vuyovich et al., 2014). Coupling ground-observed data SCA [SOURCE: Page_33]

687   and PM sensed SWE is being investigated as an alternative for enhancing the SWE [SOURCE: Page_33]

688   estimation in Mediterranean regions (e.g., Vuyovich et al., 2014; orman and Beser, 2013). [SOURCE: Page_33]

689          5.2.3. Snow albedo [SOURCE: Page_33]

690          The importance of snow albedo in Mediterranean regions has been attributed to the [SOURCE: Page_33]

691   fact that most of the snowmelt is dominated by net radiation (Sect. 4.1). A decrease in snow [SOURCE: Page_33]

692   albedo results in an increased amount of absorbed shortwave radiation and eventually, an [SOURCE: Page_33]

693   enhancement in the snowmelt. Surface snow albedo changes in Mediterranean mountain [SOURCE: Page_33]

694   regions are associated with snowpack thickness (Tekeli et al., 2006), snow grain size and [SOURCE: Page_33]

695   surface wetness (Dozier et al., 2008, 2009), and snow impurities (mainly mineral dust and [SOURCE: Page_33]

696   organic particles) (Lee and Liou, 2012). In California’s Sierra Nevada, the reduction in the [SOURCE: Page_33]

697   snow albedo during the melt season (March to April) is driven by the increase in surface [SOURCE: Page_33]

698   temperature and the increase in the deposition of absorbing aerosols. Both temperature and [SOURCE: Page_33]

699   aerosols contribute to the 61% decrease in snow albedo, and 26% of the albedo reduction was [SOURCE: Page_33]

700   attributed to the increase in the aerosol optical depth (Lee and Liou, 2012). Snow albedo can [SOURCE: Page_33]

701   be calculated by observing the incoming and reflected solar radiation at AWS. Given the lack [SOURCE: Page_33]

702   of in-situ observations, many studies have used optical remote sensing techniques. The [SOURCE: Page_33]

703   method to derive snow albedo from imaging spectrometry is well developed in literature [SOURCE: Page_33]

704   (e.g., Dozier and Painter, 2004; Seidel and Martinec 2004; Frei et al., 2012; Deems et al., [SOURCE: Page_33]

705   2013; Tedesco (ed.) 2015). In the Mediterranean context, most studies were conducted in [SOURCE: Page_33]

706   Sierra Nevada, California using MOD10A$_1$ or MODSCAG MODIS albedo products (Painter [SOURCE: Page_33]

707   et al., 2003; Dozier et al., 2009; Lee and Liou, 2012). In the Armenian Plateau, the [SOURCE: Page_33]

708   MOD10A$_1$ albedo was found to be consistent with in situ measurements in terms of [SOURCE: Page_33]

709   magnitude and temporal variability, with a small positive bias due to differences in the [SOURCE: Page_33]

710   acquisition time (Tekeli et al., 2006). On the contrary, in the Spanish Sierra Nevada, the [SOURCE: Page_33]

31 [SOURCE: Page_33]

711   coarse-resolution albedo products from MODIS and SPOT underestimated the in-situ snow [SOURCE: Page_34]

712   albedo due to the mixing effects of snow and snow-free patches in a MODIS pixel especially [SOURCE: Page_34]

713   during the melting periods (e.g., Pimentel et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_34]

714          Advances in airborne hyperspectral remote sensing have enabled the accurate [SOURCE: Page_34]

715   estimation of snow surface cover, grain size and albedo at a relatively higher cost (e.g., [SOURCE: Page_34]

716   Painter et al., 2003). Future research areas in surface snow properties can be found in Dozier [SOURCE: Page_34]

717   et al. (2009), which indicates the need to better understand the spectral characteristics of [SOURCE: Page_34]

718   snow from remote sensing sensors, to further investigate the consequences of dust and other [SOURCE: Page_34]

719   impurities on snow reflectance, and to further investigate the coupling of snow properties and [SOURCE: Page_34]

720   snowpack energy via models (e.g., Oaida et al., 2015). [SOURCE: Page_34]

721 [SOURCE: Page_34]

722          5.2.4. High resolution airborne and ground-based remote sensing [SOURCE: Page_34]

723          Over the past few years, a number of studies have used ground-based terrestrial laser [SOURCE: Page_34]

724   scanners (TLS) and airborne LiDAR to measure the snow depth at high spatial resolution [SOURCE: Page_34]

725   with decimetric accuracy (e.g., in California (Harpold et al., 2014; Kirchner et al., 2014; [SOURCE: Page_34]

726   Zheng et al., 2016), and the Spanish Pyrenees (Revuelto et al., 2014; López-Moreno et al., [SOURCE: Page_34]

727   2015)). These technologies have enabled numerous fundamental advances in our knowledge [SOURCE: Page_34]

728   of the snow depth distribution (see Sect. 5.4). They also hold potential for operational snow [SOURCE: Page_34]

729   monitoring if their costs can be mitigated; otherwise, their application will remain restricted [SOURCE: Page_34]

730   to relatively small areas, as noted in the scientific literature (<100 km^2). The NASA Airborne [SOURCE: Page_34]

731   Snow Observatory (ASO) (Painter et al., 2016) is a notable exception since it is run over [SOURCE: Page_34]

732   major water basins in the western US. ASO is composed of an imaging spectrometer and a [SOURCE: Page_34]

733   Lidar altimeter to measure the snowpack reflectance and depth from an aircraft. The SWE [SOURCE: Page_34]

734   maps are produced over 48 hours using the snow reflectance as a proxy for snow density [SOURCE: Page_34]

32 [SOURCE: Page_34]

735   (Dozier et al. 2016). Recent alternatives were proposed to determine the snow depth at high [SOURCE: Page_35]

736   resolution and at a lower cost: (1) an unmanned aircraft vehicle photogrammetric survey, and [SOURCE: Page_35]

737   (2) high-resolution stereo satellite imagery (Marti et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_35]

738      5.3. Methods of HS and SWE regionalization [SOURCE: Page_35]

739          5.3.1. Regionalization [SOURCE: Page_35]

740          Spatial interpolation (regionalization) techniques are required to estimate the [SOURCE: Page_35]

741   snowpack water equivalent at the catchment scale. Various statistical models based on the [SOURCE: Page_35]

742   terrain characteristics were proposed to compensate for the low spatial density of snow [SOURCE: Page_35]

743   course surveys and ground station observations. Spatially distributed HS estimates from [SOURCE: Page_35]

744   observations in Mediterranean mountain were represented using: linear regression models, [SOURCE: Page_35]

745   classification trees, generalized additive models (GAMs), regression tree models (e.g., [SOURCE: Page_35]

746   Molotch et al., 2005), and combined tree classification with GAM residuals (e.g., [SOURCE: Page_35]

747   López Moreno and Nogués Bravo, 2006; López-Moreno et al., 2010). Similarly, but at [SOURCE: Page_35]

748   much lower scale, the assimilation of snow cover area maps derived from time-lapse camera [SOURCE: Page_35]

749   images time series was shown to give good results in small Mediterranean pilot catchments, [SOURCE: Page_35]

750   where the snow cover is highly variable and sometimes ephemeral (Pimentel et al., 2015; [SOURCE: Page_35]

751   Revuelto et al., 2016b). [SOURCE: Page_35]

752          5.3.2. SWE reconstruction [SOURCE: Page_35]

753          Dozier et al. (2016) reviewed the methods to generate spatially distributed SWE as [SOURCE: Page_35]

754   follows: (1) spatial interpolation from ground based networks only using statistical models, [SOURCE: Page_35]

755   such as decision trees (Anderton et al., 2004); (2) constrained interpolation by remotely [SOURCE: Page_35]

756   sensed SCA (Girotto et al., 2014); (3) SWE reconstruction using snow modeling (e.g., [SOURCE: Page_35]

757   Raleigh and Lundquist, 2012; Guan et al. 2013b); and (4) reconstruction using data [SOURCE: Page_35]

33 [SOURCE: Page_35]

758   assimilation (Cortés et al., 2016) (see Sect. 6.1) and backmelt calculations (e.g., Raleigh and [SOURCE: Page_36]

759   Lundquist, 2012), which may be combined with time-lapse photography (Revuelto et al., [SOURCE: Page_36]

760   2016a). The increase in the reliance of SWE reconstruction had been motivated by the [SOURCE: Page_36]

761   assumption that knowledge of melt energy fluxes would be superior to knowledge of [SOURCE: Page_36]

762   precipitation accumulation (Cline et al., 1998; Jepsen et al., 2012; Slater et al., 2013). [SOURCE: Page_36]

763          SWE reconstruction estimates the total volume of snow based on backward [SOURCE: Page_36]

764   calculation of the amount of ablation that occurred prior to the complete removal of snow [SOURCE: Page_36]

765   (Cline et al., 1998). The SWE reconstruction process, as described by Cline et al., (1998), is [SOURCE: Page_36]

766   performed at each pixel by (1) determination of fSCA, (2) computation of the snowmelt [SOURCE: Page_36]

767   energy, and (3) determination of the initial SWE at the beginning of the melt season. [SOURCE: Page_36]

768          Several snowpack models for SWE reconstruction are constantly being tested and [SOURCE: Page_36]

769   enhanced for application in Mediterranean regions, and they range in complexity from (1) [SOURCE: Page_36]

770   simple temperature index models (Daly et al., 2000; Rice et al., 2011; Biggs and Whitaker, [SOURCE: Page_36]

771   2012), to (2) enhanced degree day models (DDMs) that account for net radiation, snow [SOURCE: Page_36]

772   albedo (e.g., Molotch and Bales, 2006), and tree cover (Biggs and Whitaker, 2012), and (3) [SOURCE: Page_36]

773   modified DDMs (based on a modified SRM (Martinec, 1975) (Tekeli et al., 2005b; ensoy [SOURCE: Page_36]

774   and Uysal, 2012), to (4) energy balance models (Cline et al., 1998; Jepsen et al., 2012; [SOURCE: Page_36]

775   Raleigh et al., 2016; Boudhar et al., 2016; Cornwell et al., 2016), and (5) models that perform [SOURCE: Page_36]

776   both forward and backward reconstruction (Raleigh and Lundquist, 2012; Revuelto et al., [SOURCE: Page_36]

777   2016a). [SOURCE: Page_36]

778          SWE reconstruction models have proven to be suitable for many Mediterranean [SOURCE: Page_36]

779   regions such as the Andes (Cornwell et al., 2016), Sierra Nevada California (Shamir and [SOURCE: Page_36]

780   Georgakakos, 2006; Rice et al., 2011; Raleigh et al., 2016), Oregon cascades (Sproles et al., [SOURCE: Page_36]

781   2013), Pyrenees (Gómez Landesa and Rango, 2002), Alps (Thirel et al., 2012), Turkey [SOURCE: Page_36]

782   (Tekeli et al., 2005b), and the Atlas Mountains (Boudhar et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_36]

34 [SOURCE: Page_36]

783           The uncertainty associated from SWE reconstruction arises from the probable error [SOURCE: Page_37]

784   propagation in model forcing especially in areas where dense observing network are not [SOURCE: Page_37]

785   available (Lundquist et al., 2015b; Dozier et al., 2016). Another source of uncertainty is [SOURCE: Page_37]

786   attributed to the retrieval and spatial resolution of SCA and fSCA from imaging spectrometer [SOURCE: Page_37]

787   (e.g., AVHRR and MODIS) (Sect. 5.2) and return time period (e.g., 16 days for Landsat) [SOURCE: Page_37]

788   (Slater et al., 2013). The Sentinel-2 mission with its 5 days repeat cycle offers the prospect of [SOURCE: Page_37]

789   improving the SWE reconstruction results in regions where the snow cover variability is high [SOURCE: Page_37]

790   (Marti et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_37]

791           SWE reconstruction methods based on the use of depletion curves that relate [SOURCE: Page_37]

792   fractional snow cover area to average SWE should consider that there is a hysteresis in the [SOURCE: Page_37]

793   SWE-fSCA relationship (Luce and Tarboton, 2004; Magand et al., 2014; Gascoin et al, [SOURCE: Page_37]

794   2015). In fact, a given snow cover fraction generally corresponds to a smaller snow mass at [SOURCE: Page_37]

795   the beginning of the snow season to that found at the end of the season. This is due to the [SOURCE: Page_37]

796   snowpack evolution along the season, where snow wind redistribution and snowmelt [SOURCE: Page_37]

797   occurring at preferential locations, tend to increase the heterogeneity in the SWE spatial [SOURCE: Page_37]

798   distribution. [SOURCE: Page_37]

799           The success of the spatial interpolation techniques for the estimation of HS and SWE [SOURCE: Page_37]

800   should encourage the implementation of regular snow surveys and automatic snow stations in [SOURCE: Page_37]

801   less monitored mountain regions, such as the Andes, Atlas Mountains, and Mount Lebanon. [SOURCE: Page_37]

802   In the absence of ground measurements, methods based on SCA data assimilation into an [SOURCE: Page_37]

803   energy balance snow model are the most promising since they rely on publicly available [SOURCE: Page_37]

804   remote sensing and climate datasets (reanalyses) with global coverage (e.g., Kapnick and [SOURCE: Page_37]

805   Delworth, 2013). [SOURCE: Page_37]

35 [SOURCE: Page_37]

806      5.4. Snowpack spatial variability [SOURCE: Page_38]

807          5.4.1. Snow height spatial variability [SOURCE: Page_38]

808          Results from the aforementioned studies indicate that in Mediterranean mountain [SOURCE: Page_38]

809   regions, the spatial distribution of the snow height is driven by meteorological forcing [SOURCE: Page_38]

810   (López-Moreno 2005; Svoma, 2011; Mizukami and Smith, 2012; Bormann et al., 2013; Luce [SOURCE: Page_38]

811   et al., 2014) and topography (elevation, slope, aspect and related radiation parameters) (e.g., [SOURCE: Page_38]

812   Elder et al., 1998; Anderton et al., 2004; López Moreno and Nogués Bravo, 2006; Rice et [SOURCE: Page_38]

813   al., 2011; Molotch and Meromy, 2014; Revuelto et al., 2014). Canopy interception (e.g., [SOURCE: Page_38]

814   López-Moreno and Latron, 2008; Revuelto et al., 2015; Zheng et al., 2016) plays a secondary [SOURCE: Page_38]

815   role in comparison with other snow regions because most of the snowpack accumulates [SOURCE: Page_38]

816   above the tree line in many Mediterranean mountains. Forest regions are present at mid- [SOURCE: Page_38]

817   altitude mountain regions (2000-2600 m a.s.l) in California (e.g., Rice and Bales, 2010; [SOURCE: Page_38]

818   Musselman et al., 2012), Oregon (Kostadinov and Lookingbill, 2015), and the Pyrenees (e.g., [SOURCE: Page_38]

819   Gascoin et al., 2015). Snow redistribution due to wind is probably an important process [SOURCE: Page_38]

820   (Gascoin et al., 2013), and it is one of the less explored fields of research in Mediterranean [SOURCE: Page_38]

821   regions. This may be due to the lack of accurate information needed to create reliable wind [SOURCE: Page_38]

822   fields over complex topography. Snow models run using high-resolution meteorological [SOURCE: Page_38]

823   forcing are still unable to capture wind snow redistribution, which usually occurs at the sub- [SOURCE: Page_38]

824   pixel scale (e.g., Quéno et al., 2016). However, the wind redistribution may be less important [SOURCE: Page_38]

825   in Mediterranean mountains than in colder regions due to the higher snowpack densification [SOURCE: Page_38]

826   rates. Table 2 summarizes the contribution of climate forcing and mountain controls [SOURCE: Page_38]

827   potentials in explaining snow distribution and depth, and the level of uncertainty in [SOURCE: Page_38]

828   Mediterranean mountain regions. [SOURCE: Page_38]

36 [SOURCE: Page_38]

829          For example, in Sierra Nevada California, Elder et al., (1998) obtained a model that [SOURCE: Page_39]

830   could explain up to 70% of the observed variance in HS using the elevation, net radiation and [SOURCE: Page_39]

831   slope as predictors. According to Molotch and Meromy, (2014), snow cover persistence is [SOURCE: Page_39]

832   driven by mountain controls where elevation (the most explanatory variable) and climate [SOURCE: Page_39]

833   controls of precipitation and temperature determined most of the snow variability. Vegetation [SOURCE: Page_39]

834   and slope ranked second in explaining part of the snow cover variability, whereas shortwave [SOURCE: Page_39]

835   solar radiation and the terrain aspect were of tertiary importance. Similar findings were [SOURCE: Page_39]

836   reported in the Spanish Pyrenees, which indicates that elevation and solar radiation explain a [SOURCE: Page_39]

837   high percentage of the variance in HS (Anderton et al., 2004; López Moreno and [SOURCE: Page_39]

838   Nogués Bravo, 2005). [SOURCE: Page_39]

839          Similar findings based on high-resolution airborne lidar were reported from an [SOURCE: Page_39]

840   experiment over a micro-scale snow dominated basin (with an elevation range of 1500–3300 [SOURCE: Page_39]

841   m) in the Sierra Nevada, California; the experiment indicated that 43% of snow-depth [SOURCE: Page_39]

842   variability can be explained by elevation, and another 14% is related to the slope, aspect and [SOURCE: Page_39]

843   canopy penetration fraction (Zheng et al., 2016). A ground-based terrestrial laser scanner in [SOURCE: Page_39]

844   the Pyrenees demonstrated that the high resolution topographic position index and maximum [SOURCE: Page_39]

845   upwind slope are more statistically significant ( <0.05) in explaining the intra-annual snow [SOURCE: Page_39]

846   variability compared to the elevation and slope (Revuelto et al., 2014). Snow variability at [SOURCE: Page_39]

847   this scale is further influenced by mountain curvature, whereas the aspect and the computed [SOURCE: Page_39]

848   incoming radiation were found to be less statistically significant when correlated with intra- [SOURCE: Page_39]

849   annual snow variability (Revuelto et al., 2014). [SOURCE: Page_39]

850          Despite these encouraging results, to date, there is no accepted universal law to derive [SOURCE: Page_39]

851   HS or SWE from a set of predictors that can be obtained anywhere. This is because the link [SOURCE: Page_39]

852   between the terrain parameters and the snow distribution is not fully explained at the small [SOURCE: Page_39]

853   scale (e.g., Molotch et al., 2005; López-Moreno et al., 2010, 2015; Revuelto et al., 2014; [SOURCE: Page_39]

37 [SOURCE: Page_39]

854   Zheng et al., 2016). This quest is hindered in particular by (1) the high inter-annual snow [SOURCE: Page_40]

855   variability in Mediterranean regions, since most studies rely on 1-2 years of sporadic [SOURCE: Page_40]

856   observations (e.g., Anderton et al., 2004; López-Moreno et al., 2010; Zheng et al., 2016), and [SOURCE: Page_40]

857   (2) the increase in the scale dependency of the model results (e.g., the importance of [SOURCE: Page_40]

858   elevation) as the grid cell size increases (López-Moreno et al., 2010). [SOURCE: Page_40]

859           The presence of vegetation, especially forest, adds to the ambiguity in explaining [SOURCE: Page_40]

860   topographic control because it modifies meteorological variables, such as wind and the [SOURCE: Page_40]

861   incoming radiation, with difference intensities, which depends on the canopy density, trees [SOURCE: Page_40]

862   trunks and crow size (e.g., Musselman et al., 2012; Harpold et al., 2014; Zheng et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_40]

863   The snow depth can be significantly reduced by 20% to 80% in forested areas compared with [SOURCE: Page_40]

864   open sites due to interception and the sublimation or melting of the intercepted snow [SOURCE: Page_40]

865   (Revuelto et al., 2015; Szczypta et al., 2015). Information on the impact of vegetation cover [SOURCE: Page_40]

866   on snow interception in Mediterranean mountain regions and elsewhere (Varhola et al., 2010) [SOURCE: Page_40]

867   remains limited and warrants future research (López-Moreno and Latron, 2008; Musselman [SOURCE: Page_40]

868   et al., 2012; Raleigh et al., 2013; Revuelto et al., 2015; Zheng et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_40]

869   Table 2. Main variables influencing the snow depth spatial distribution Variable                                             Control Level Elevation [1-5, 7-9]                                 Medium to very high Slope [1-5, 7-9]                                     Low to high (micro); High (meso-macro) Curvature [5, 8-9]                                   High at micro Exposure [3-5, 7-8]                                  Low at micro to medium at the micro scale Radiation [1-3, 5, 7-9]                              Low to medium (micro); High (macro) Relative elevation [2, 5, 8]                         Low to very high Upwind slope [3, 5, 7, 9]                            High at micro Canopy interception [4, 6]                           Medium to high 870   Radiation: potential incoming solar radiation; Relative elevation (inc. topographic position index (TPI) and 871   combined TPI); Upwind slope: maximum upwind slope. 872   Scale (micro 1–102 km^2; meso 102–104 km^2; macro > 104 km^2) 873   Sources: [1] Elder et al., 1998; [2] López Moreno and Nogués Bravo, 2005, 2006; [3] Anderton et al., 2004; 874   [4] Zheng et al., 2016; [5-6] Revuelto et al., 2014, 2015; [7] Molotch et al., 2005; [8] López-Moreno et al., 875   2010; and [9] López -Moreno et al., 2015. 876 [SOURCE: Page_40]

38 [SOURCE: Page_40]

877            5.4.2. Snowpack density [SOURCE: Page_41]

878            Over most parts of the maritime US, the snowpack density was found to be highly [SOURCE: Page_41]

879   correlated with total precipitation (Svoma, 2011). The variability in snow densities is also [SOURCE: Page_41]

880   driven by the average air temperature during days with no snowfall, the mean snowfall [SOURCE: Page_41]

881   density, the fractional precipitation that falls as snow (Svoma, 2011) and melt-refreeze events [SOURCE: Page_41]

882   (Bormann et al., 2013). At the slope scale, densification processes are further influenced by [SOURCE: Page_41]

883   solar radiation, slope, vegetation cover, and wind exposure (Bormann et al., 2013, Elder et [SOURCE: Page_41]

884   al., 1998). [SOURCE: Page_41]

885            However, the observed spatial variability of the snowpack density in Mediterranean [SOURCE: Page_41]

886   regions is much lower than the spatial variability of the snow height (Mizukami and Perica, [SOURCE: Page_41]

887   2008; López-Moreno et al., 2013b). As a result, the number of density measurements [SOURCE: Page_41]

888   required to derive the SWE may be lower than the number of HS measurements. This has [SOURCE: Page_41]

889   important implications for the monitoring of the snowpack since density measurements in the [SOURCE: Page_41]

890   field are time consuming. Similarly, year-to-year changes are significantly higher for the HS [SOURCE: Page_41]

891   than for the snowpack density (Mizukami and Perica, 2008; Bormann et al., 2013). However, [SOURCE: Page_41]

892   the density should be carefully measured at the start and end of each season when its intra- [SOURCE: Page_41]

893   annual variance is maximal (Sturm et al., 2010; López-Moreno et al., 2013b; Bormann et al., [SOURCE: Page_41]

894   2013; Trujillo and Molotch, 2014). The low inter-annual variability of snow density also [SOURCE: Page_41]

895   holds potentials in Mediterranean regions. Climatological values of snow density can be [SOURCE: Page_41]

896   estimated with confidence using few years of measurements (Anderton et al., 2004; [SOURCE: Page_41]

897   Mizukami and Perica, 2008; Meromy et al., 2013), combined with regular HS measurements, [SOURCE: Page_41]

898   to estimate the SWE for hydrological applications in mountains (Mizukami and Perica, [SOURCE: Page_41]

899   2008). [SOURCE: Page_41]

39 [SOURCE: Page_41]

- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation
