## 900          5.4.3. SWE

901          Long-term records of SWE (> 50 years) indicate that the maximum snow [SOURCE: Page_42]

902   accumulation is higher and the snow season is shorter in the Oregon Cascades and the [SOURCE: Page_42]

903   California Sierra Nevada than in continental mountain ranges (Trujillo and Molotch, 2014). [SOURCE: Page_42]

904   In the western US, the April 1st SWE (a proxy of the annual peak of SWE) and the snow [SOURCE: Page_42]

905   residence time (SRT) are highly correlated with daily temperature and precipitation (Luce et [SOURCE: Page_42]

906   al., 2014). Hence, the observed decrease in SWE in this region was linked to the regional [SOURCE: Page_42]

907   increase in temperature, and the results were qualitatively consistent with observed trends in [SOURCE: Page_42]

908   temperature and precipitation at nearby stations (Mote, 2003). In the southern Italian Alps, [SOURCE: Page_42]

909   the SWE average and variances are known to a good degree of approximation if continuous [SOURCE: Page_42]

910   information on the accumulated SWE, snow depth, and density are known (Bocchiola and [SOURCE: Page_42]

911   Rosso, 2007). Smaller scale studies in California and Central Andes indicate a higher [SOURCE: Page_42]

912   influence of the slope and maximum upwind slope (Molotch et al., 2005; Welch et al., 2013; [SOURCE: Page_42]

913   Ayala et al., 2014). The influence of the theoretical incoming radiation increased during the [SOURCE: Page_42]

914   melt season, which is in agreement with energy balance model studies (Molotch et al., 2005; [SOURCE: Page_42]

915   Ayala et al., 2014) (Sect. 4). We do not detail the spatial variability of the SWE since it is [SOURCE: Page_42]

916   largely inherited from the HS, as presented above. [SOURCE: Page_42]

917   6. Snowmelt hydrology and hydrogeology [SOURCE: Page_42]

918          Once snowmelt occurs, the snowpack water is channeled through surface outflows [SOURCE: Page_42]

919   and streams, and it undergoes evapotranspiration, subsurface flow and groundwater recharge [SOURCE: Page_42]

920   via deep percolation (e.g., Knowles and Cayan, 2004; Franz et al., 2010; Lundquist and [SOURCE: Page_42]

921   Loheide, 2011; Smith et al., 2013; Manning et al., 2012; Godsey et al., 2014). Given the [SOURCE: Page_42]

922   spatio-temporal variability of the climatic conditions that were highlighted in the previous [SOURCE: Page_42]

923   sections, the closure of the hydrologic budget in Mediterranean snow-dominated basins [SOURCE: Page_42]

40 [SOURCE: Page_42]

924   remains challenging (e.g., Ralph et al., 2016). In addition, several authors point to a limited [SOURCE: Page_43]

925   understanding of surface and sub-surface hydrologic processes in mountains (Bales et al., [SOURCE: Page_43]

926   2006; Smith et al., 2013). [SOURCE: Page_43]

927      6.1. Snowmelt modeling [SOURCE: Page_43]

928          6.1.1. Snow melt models [SOURCE: Page_43]

929          Different snowmelt models were applied in the Mediterranean context with [SOURCE: Page_43]

930   significantly varying levels of details between the models. Following DeWalle and Rango [SOURCE: Page_43]

931   (2008), these models can be classified under three categories: (1) statistical snowmelt-runoff [SOURCE: Page_43]

932   methods (e.g., Stewart et al., 2005), (2) temperature index or degree day models (TIM/DDM) [SOURCE: Page_43]

933   (e.g., Null et al., 2010), and (3) physically based distributed-snowmelt models or energy [SOURCE: Page_43]

934   balance models (EBM) (e.g., Herrero et al., (2009) in the Spanish Sierra Nevada, ensoy et [SOURCE: Page_43]

935   al., (2006) in the Karasu basin, Turkey, and Mernild et al. (2016a) in the Andes). The [SOURCE: Page_43]

936   distinction between these boundaries seems to be fading in the literature as there is a [SOURCE: Page_43]

937   tendency towards the use of heterogeneous modeling approaches, and a continuum now [SOURCE: Page_43]

938   exists between the three categories. Martelloni et al., (2013) proposed and tested a modeling [SOURCE: Page_43]

939   scheme, over the Italian Apennines, that is considered as an intermediate approach between [SOURCE: Page_43]

940   temperature index and physically based models. Sproles et al., (2016) used a modified [SOURCE: Page_43]

941   snowmelt runoff model (SRM), which was run using MODIS SCA data, to investigate [SOURCE: Page_43]

942   snowmelt forecasts in the data-poor regions of the Chilean Andes. [SOURCE: Page_43]

943          Major sources of uncertainty in snow melt simulation arise from (1) the error in the [SOURCE: Page_43]

944   input data (particularly precipitation), (2) reliance on DDM (due to limited information on [SOURCE: Page_43]

945   incoming and reflected radiation and fluxes), which usually translates into the lack of [SOURCE: Page_43]

946   accounting of snow sublimation, and (3) the model parameterization regarding rain/snow [SOURCE: Page_43]

947   separation and turbulent fluxes (Franz et al., 2010; He et al., 2011; Raleigh and Lundquist, [SOURCE: Page_43]

41 [SOURCE: Page_43]

948   2012; Slater et al., 2013; Avanzi et al., 2014). Snow model intercomparison studies in [SOURCE: Page_44]

949   Mediterranean mountain regions (Franz et al., 2010; Slater et al., 2013) and elsewhere [SOURCE: Page_44]

950   (Molotch and Margulis, 2008; Essery et al., 2013; Bavera et al., 2014) indicate that while [SOURCE: Page_44]

951   very large differences can exist between models (different melt algorithms), proper [SOURCE: Page_44]

952   simulation of snowmelt is highly associated with the proper parameterization of models (e.g., [SOURCE: Page_44]

953   Smith et al., 2013) and the model’s ability to solve SWE with high confidence (e.g., Franz et [SOURCE: Page_44]

954   al., 2010). To the best of our knowledge the only snow-hydrological model comparison [SOURCE: Page_44]

955   studies, which include Mediterranean catchments, are the studies of Franz et al., (2010) and [SOURCE: Page_44]

956   Valéry et al. (2014a, 2014b). We believe that there is a need for testing and comparing [SOURCE: Page_44]

957   different snow models and carrying out meaningful intercomparison exercises across [SOURCE: Page_44]

958   different Mediterranean mountain regions. The reader is advised to look at model [SOURCE: Page_44]

959   parameterizations and sensitivity (Clark et al., 2011; Garcia et al., 2013), the intercomparison [SOURCE: Page_44]

960   in forested snow regions (e.g., Essery et al., 2009; Rutter et al., 2009), the influence of soil [SOURCE: Page_44]

961   moisture response on snow distribution and melt (Bales et al., 2011; Kerkez et al., 2012; [SOURCE: Page_44]

962   Harpold et al., 2015), single model SRM multi-site comparison (e.g., Martinec and Rango, [SOURCE: Page_44]

963   1986; Seidel and Martinec, 2004; DeWalle and Rango, 2008), ensemble model simulations [SOURCE: Page_44]

964   (e.g., Franz et al., 2010; Essery et al., 2013), and the snow model intercomparison project [SOURCE: Page_44]

965   (MIP) (e.g., DMIP (Smith et al., 2013) and SNOWMIP$_2$ (Essery et al., 2009)). [SOURCE: Page_44]

966          6.1.2. Large-scale land surface models and assimilation techniques [SOURCE: Page_44]

967          The modeling of large-scale distributed cryospheric processes had been made possible [SOURCE: Page_44]

968   through the application of the following: (1) finer scale land surface models with snow [SOURCE: Page_44]

969   schemes (e.g., Boone et al. 2004; Livneh et al., 2010; Brun et al., 2013; Magand et al., 2014; [SOURCE: Page_44]

970   Singh et al., 2015); (2) physically based simulations using mesoscale WRF model (Caldwell [SOURCE: Page_44]

971   et al., 2009; Pavelsky et al., 2011, 2012; Wayand et al., 2013; Liou et al., 2013; Franz et al., [SOURCE: Page_44]

972   2014; Oaida et al., 2015); (3) blending snow sensor observations and remote sensing data [SOURCE: Page_44]

42 [SOURCE: Page_44]

973   with snowmelt model simulations (e.g., Guan et al. 2013a; Rittger et al., 2016), and using [SOURCE: Page_45]

974   data assimilation (e.g., Franz et al., 2014; Girotto et al., 2014) of ground and remote sensing [SOURCE: Page_45]

975   data with Land Surface Model (Zaitchik and Rodell, 2009; Hancock et al., 2013) and Snow [SOURCE: Page_45]

976   Data Assimilation System (SNODAS) (Guan et al., 2013a; Vuyovich et al., 2014); and (4) [SOURCE: Page_45]

977   reanalysis of ground data (Gottardi et al., 2012; Avanzi et al., 2014), remote sensing data [SOURCE: Page_45]

978   (Margulis et al., 2016), and land surface data (e.g., Durand et al., 2009; Vidal et al., 2010; [SOURCE: Page_45]

979   Rousselot et al., 2012; Rutz et al., 2014). [SOURCE: Page_45]

980          While such techniques can capture large-scale variability in the cryospheric system, [SOURCE: Page_45]

981   most of these techniques are still hindered by (1) limited ground data and dependence on the [SOURCE: Page_45]

982   representativeness of the observation network (e.g., Livneh et al., 2010; Guan et al., 2013a; [SOURCE: Page_45]

983   Balsamo et al., 2015; Dozier et al., 2016); (2) uncertainties in the quality of forcing data [SOURCE: Page_45]

984   (surface meteorological and radiative forcing) (Livneh et al., 2010; Pavelsky et al., 2011; [SOURCE: Page_45]

985   Gottardi et al., 2012; Liou et al., 2013; Guan et al., 2013a); (3) model parameterization and [SOURCE: Page_45]

986   the number of model simplifications of physical phenomena (Livneh et al., 2010); (4) [SOURCE: Page_45]

987   limitations in mountain environments (Gottardi et al., 2012; Wayand et al., 2013; Wrzesien et [SOURCE: Page_45]

988   al., 2015), which are attributed to the downscaling of surface forcing over topographically [SOURCE: Page_45]

989   complex areas (Livneh et al., 2010; Guan et al., 2013a); (5) fSCA data retrieval gaps due to [SOURCE: Page_45]

990   cloud contamination (Guan et al., 2013a); (6) lack in accounting for the spatial variability [SOURCE: Page_45]

991   snow of albedo (Livneh et al., 2010; Guan et al., 2013a); (7) accounting for canopy/forest [SOURCE: Page_45]

992   cover (Livneh et al., 2010; Guan et al., 2013a); and (8) tendency towards reporting earlier [SOURCE: Page_45]

993   snowmelt/depletion (Pavelsky et al., 2011; Wrzesien et al., 2015) and higher biases observed [SOURCE: Page_45]

994   during snow depletion (Wrzesien et al., 2015) ablation seasons (Guan et al., 2013a). [SOURCE: Page_45]

995 [SOURCE: Page_45]

43 [SOURCE: Page_45]

996      6.2. Snowmelt contribution to runoff and groundwater [SOURCE: Page_46]

997          While rainfall defines most of the hydrograph shape in the mid to low mountain [SOURCE: Page_46]

998   regions, the streamflow in higher elevation areas (typically above 2000 m a.s.l.) is dominated [SOURCE: Page_46]

999   by snowmelt (López-Moreno and García-Ruiz 2004; Wayand et al., 2013; Jepsen et al. [SOURCE: Page_46]

1000   2016a). In the western US, snowmelt-dominated rivers reach their highest sustained flow [SOURCE: Page_46]

1001   during the spring melt season, whereas rain-dominated rivers achieve their highest sustained [SOURCE: Page_46]

1002   flows during the winter rainy season (Lundquist and Cayan, 2002). Streamflow was estimated [SOURCE: Page_46]

1003   to peak 2-4 weeks earlier in transitional rain–snow-dominated basins compared to snow- [SOURCE: Page_46]

1004   dominated basins (Ashfaq et al., 2013; Liu et al., 2013). Processes controlling the water [SOURCE: Page_46]

1005   transfer through overland, subsurface and groundwater flow are reasonably well understood [SOURCE: Page_46]

1006   (e.g., Jefferson et al., 2008; Tague and Grant, 2009; Smith et al., 2013; Wayand et al., 2013). [SOURCE: Page_46]

1007   However, the effect of the snowpack dynamics on those processes is one of the foremost [SOURCE: Page_46]

1008   challenges in the hydrology of Mediterranean mountains. [SOURCE: Page_46]

1009          6.2.1. Snowmelt runoff [SOURCE: Page_46]

1010           The simulation of the streamflow in snow-dominated Mediterranean regions was [SOURCE: Page_46]

1011   successfully achieved using lumped rainfall-runoff models with a snowmelt routine (e.g., [SOURCE: Page_46]

1012   Karpouzos et al., 2011), a distributed statistical model that combines remote sensing SCA and [SOURCE: Page_46]

1013   ground observation (e.g., Gómez Landesa and Rango, 2002; Powell et al., 2011; Akyurek et [SOURCE: Page_46]

1014   al., 2011; Biggs and Whitaker, 2012), and a physically based distributed hydrologic model [SOURCE: Page_46]

1015   that balances both surface energy and water budgets and accounts for snowmelt using [SOURCE: Page_46]

1016   snowpack energy balance routines, such as the variable infiltration capacity (VIC) model [SOURCE: Page_46]

1017   (Maurer et al., 2007) and the Distributed Hydrology Soil Vegetation Model (DHSVM) (e.g., [SOURCE: Page_46]

1018   Wayand et al., 2013; Cristea et al., 2014). Apart from the modeling approach, the relationship [SOURCE: Page_46]

1019   between snowmelt and streamflow in Mediterranean snow-dominated regions has also been [SOURCE: Page_46]

44 [SOURCE: Page_46]

1020   successfully addressed by the empirical analysis of streamflow data against observed SWE [SOURCE: Page_47]

1021   (Lundquist et al., 2004) and tracer tests (Liu et al., 2013). [SOURCE: Page_47]

1022          There is a global agreement that the total annual runoff volume will decrease, in [SOURCE: Page_47]

1023   regions such as California, under a warming climate (Jepsen et al., 2016a). Changes in snow- [SOURCE: Page_47]

1024   fed streamflow volume are controlled by the (1) annual snow mass (total snowfall) and melt [SOURCE: Page_47]

1025   rates (e.g., Lundquist et al., 2005; Franz and Karsten, 2013; Morán-Tejeda et al., 2014; [SOURCE: Page_47]

1026   Godsey et al., 2014), and (2) subsurface processes (Liu et al., 2013; Jepsen et al., 2016a). [SOURCE: Page_47]

1027   Controls driven by (1) soil moisture and water holding capacity (Costa-Cabral et al., 2013), [SOURCE: Page_47]

1028   (2) vegetation cover (Biggs and Whitaker, 2012; Cristea et al., 2014), (3) evapotranspiration [SOURCE: Page_47]

1029   (Lundquist and Loheide, 2011; Goulden et al., 2012; Godsey et al., 2014), and (4) [SOURCE: Page_47]

1030   groundwater storage (Godsey et al., 2014) are usually site specific and vary depending on soil [SOURCE: Page_47]

1031   type (e.g., soil water holding capacity), and the groundwater storage capacity and flow time [SOURCE: Page_47]

1032   (e.g., the low capacity and faster travel time in Karst). A study carried in a snow–rain [SOURCE: Page_47]

1033   transition mountain region in the Southern Sierra Nevada, California indicated that [SOURCE: Page_47]

1034   streamflow generation is controlled by subsurface flow (average relative contribution to [SOURCE: Page_47]

1035   streamflow discharge was greater than 60%), snowmelt runoff including rain on snow (less [SOURCE: Page_47]

1036   than 40%), and fall storm runoff (less than 7%), whereas soil water in the unsaturated zone [SOURCE: Page_47]

1037   and regional groundwater were not significant contributors to streamflow (Liu et al., 2013). [SOURCE: Page_47]

1038   In the Spanish Pyrenees, using a single multiple regression model, the contribution of [SOURCE: Page_47]

1039   snowpack to spring runoff was estimated to be a 42% for the 1955-2000 period (López- [SOURCE: Page_47]

1040   Moreno and García-Ruiz, 2004). In the Anti Lebanon Mountain, the karst formation has a [SOURCE: Page_47]

1041   short-term influence characterized by an intra-annual patterns of fast spring discharges [SOURCE: Page_47]

1042   (Koeniger et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_47]

1043          Regional long-term trends in the snowmelt-generated streamflow in California are [SOURCE: Page_47]

1044   believed to be controlled by long-term decadal changes and spring warming temperature [SOURCE: Page_47]

45 [SOURCE: Page_47]

1045   trends (e.g., Stewart et al., 2005; Maurer et al., 2007). Whereas, the inter-annual variations of [SOURCE: Page_48]

1046   snowmelt and streamflow timing are driven by regional temperature fluctuations and [SOURCE: Page_48]

1047   precipitation anomalies (Stewart et al., 2005). It is clear that temperature and precipitation [SOURCE: Page_48]

1048   alone cannot explain the entire variability in snowmelt onset and streamflow peak timing and [SOURCE: Page_48]

1049   that changes are also influenced by elevation (Maurer et al., 2007; Biggs and Whitaker, 2012; [SOURCE: Page_48]

1050   Wayand et al., 2013) and shifts in snowfall-snowmelt patterns in regions, such as California [SOURCE: Page_48]

1051   (Godsey et al. 2014), and the Spanish Sierra Nevada (Morán-Tejeda et al., 2014). In [SOURCE: Page_48]

1052   Mediterranean regions, a shift in streamflow timing would have profound implications on [SOURCE: Page_48]

1053   water management by reducing the available water resources in late spring and summer when [SOURCE: Page_48]

1054   the precipitation is low (e.g., Stewart et al., 2005; Tanaka et al., 2006; López-Moreno et al., [SOURCE: Page_48]

1055   2008b; Vicuña et al., 2011). In California, changes in snowmelt and streamflow onset timing, [SOURCE: Page_48]

1056   at the micro- and mesoscale, were found to respond non-linearly to the increase in [SOURCE: Page_48]

1057   temperature (Lundquist and Flint, 2006). Snowmelt-driven streamflow timing, at smaller [SOURCE: Page_48]

1058   scales, is strongly dominated by solar radiation and the combined effect of solar radiation [SOURCE: Page_48]

1059   exposure (a function of aspect, elevation and time of the year) and temperature (a function of [SOURCE: Page_48]

1060   elevation and shading) (Lundquist and Flint, 2006). These results highlight the need to [SOURCE: Page_48]

1061   account for solar radiation, meteorological forcing, and topography when addressing [SOURCE: Page_48]

1062   snowmelt and streamflow responses in Mediterranean-like regions (Wayand et al., 2013). [SOURCE: Page_48]

1063   Diurnal snow-dominated streamflow patterns are also sensitive to the basin size (Lundquist et [SOURCE: Page_48]

1064   al., 2005). At the micro-scale (< 30 km^2), travel times through the snowpack dominate [SOURCE: Page_48]

1065   streamflow timing, whereas in mesoscale basins (>200 km^2), streamflow peaks are more [SOURCE: Page_48]

1066   consistent, with little or no variation, due to snowpack heterogeneity and the longer travel [SOURCE: Page_48]

1067   percolation times through deeper snowpacks and stream channels. [SOURCE: Page_48]

46 [SOURCE: Page_48]

1068          6.2.2.   Snowpack control on soil moisture and evapotranspiration [SOURCE: Page_49]

1069          The role of the soil water holding capacity in controlling soil moistures and [SOURCE: Page_49]

1070   evapotranspiration is usually site specific (Christensen et al., 2008; Maurer et al., 2010; Bales [SOURCE: Page_49]

1071   et al., 2011; Schlaepfer et al., 2012; Tague and Peng, 2013; Harpold et al., 2015; Jepsen et al., [SOURCE: Page_49]

1072   2016a, 2016b). The interaction between snowmelt and soil moisture is subject to soil physical [SOURCE: Page_49]

1073   properties (texture) and soil depth (Schlaepfer et al., 2012; Bales et al., 2011; Harpold, 2016). [SOURCE: Page_49]

1074          At present, little information is available on soil water holding capacities at high [SOURCE: Page_49]

1075   elevations (e.g., Christensen et al., 2008; Bales et al., 2011; Costa-Cabral et al., 2013). [SOURCE: Page_49]

1076   Information on the soil water potential and water table depth is useful to explain the runoff [SOURCE: Page_49]

1077   generation processes (Latron and Gallart, 2008). An increase in temperature would not only [SOURCE: Page_49]

1078   reduce the snow accumulation but also increase the soil water storage and evapotranspiration [SOURCE: Page_49]

1079   in snow-dominated basins (Maurer et al., 2010; Tague and Peng, 2013; Wu et al., 2015). The [SOURCE: Page_49]

1080   acquisition of soil moisture data in snow-dominated mountain regions would aid a better [SOURCE: Page_49]

1081   understanding and forecasting snowmelt runoff (Kerkez et al., 2012). [SOURCE: Page_49]

1082          A    number     of   studies   investigated   evapotranspiration    in   snow-influenced [SOURCE: Page_49]

1083   Mediterranean mountains, mostly in California Sierra Nevada (Leydecker and Melack, 2000; [SOURCE: Page_49]

1084   Dettinger et al. 2004; Bales et al., 2011; Lundquist and Loheide, 2011; Tague and Peng, [SOURCE: Page_49]

1085   2013; Costa-Cabral et al., 2013; Goulden and Bales, 2014; Jepsen et al., 2016b; Harpold, [SOURCE: Page_49]

1086   2016), Sierra Nevada Spain (Aguilar et al., 2010), and Southern Italy (Senatore et al., 2011). [SOURCE: Page_49]

1087   The interplay between snowmelt and evapotranspiration was most of the time addressed [SOURCE: Page_49]

1088   using modeling approaches that do not explicitly account for snowpack dynamics (e.g., [SOURCE: Page_49]

1089   Aguilar et al., 2010; Lundquist and Loheide, 2011; Tague and Peng, 2013; Jepsen et al., [SOURCE: Page_49]

1090   2016a). [SOURCE: Page_49]

47 [SOURCE: Page_49]

1091          In Mediterranean regions, high-elevation areas are usually snowmelt-dominated [SOURCE: Page_50]

1092   whereas lower-regions are evapotranspiration/infiltration-dominated (Lundquist and Cayan, [SOURCE: Page_50]

1093   2002). Evapotranspiration tends to be low during the winter season through the beginning of [SOURCE: Page_50]

1094   the melt season due to the presence of the snow cover and at late summer and autumn due to [SOURCE: Page_50]

1095   soil dryness. During the melt season, the combined effect of increasing air temperatures and [SOURCE: Page_50]

1096   solar radiation tends to accelerate snowmelt, enhance water availability in soil, and increase [SOURCE: Page_50]

1097   surface temperature, which results in increased evapotranspiration (Leydecker and Melack, [SOURCE: Page_50]

1098   2000). The inter-annual evapotranspiration is controlled by elevation and aspect, which [SOURCE: Page_50]

1099   define the amount of incoming solar radiation (Goulden et al., 2012; Lundquist and Loheide, [SOURCE: Page_50]

1100   2011). Evapotranspiration in mid-altitude regions is usually water-limited, i.e., controlled by [SOURCE: Page_50]

1101   the precipitation (Christensen et al., 2008; Lundquist and Loheide, 2011), whereas in higher [SOURCE: Page_50]

1102   snow-dominated regions, it is relatively energy-limited, i.e., it responds more strongly to [SOURCE: Page_50]

1103   temperature variations (Christensen et al., 2008; Lundquist and Loheide, 2011; Schlaepfer et [SOURCE: Page_50]

1104   al., 2012; Godsey et al., 2014; Jepsen et al., 2016a). Based on a number of papers reported in [SOURCE: Page_50]

1105   Jepsen et al., (2016a), the evapotranspiration in Sierra Nevada California under a warming [SOURCE: Page_50]

1106   climate, has an overall tendency to increase (medium confidence) in snow-dominated [SOURCE: Page_50]

1107   regions, and some regions are either susceptible to warming or expected to experience a [SOURCE: Page_50]

1108   slight decrease. [SOURCE: Page_50]

1109          6.2.3. Groundwater recharge [SOURCE: Page_50]

1110          Groundwater studies in Mediterranean mountain are most of the time presented at the [SOURCE: Page_50]

1111   basin scale and they provide little information on the link between snowpack dynamics and [SOURCE: Page_50]

1112   groundwater processes. Groundwater recharge in snow-dominated regions is dominated by [SOURCE: Page_50]

1113   the timing of the snowmelt (early or late spring) and the subsurface flow (Tague and Grant, [SOURCE: Page_50]

1114   2009). In groundwater-dominated watersheds, the aquifer storage and the slow recession can [SOURCE: Page_50]

1115   help in sustaining discharge during the summer dry periods even under a negative yearly [SOURCE: Page_50]

48 [SOURCE: Page_50]

1116   water balance (Jefferson et al., 2008). Despite its importance, the lack of extended studies has [SOURCE: Page_51]

1117   resulted in the availability of negligible information on the extent of snowpack control on [SOURCE: Page_51]

1118   groundwater resources in most Mediterranean mountains (e.g., Palmer et al., 2007; Lowry et [SOURCE: Page_51]

1119   al., 2010, 2011; Liu et al., 2013; Valdés-Pineda et al., 2014). The separation between rain- [SOURCE: Page_51]

1120   fed and snow-fed groundwater recharge is still incomplete due to a number of factors: (1) [SOURCE: Page_51]

1121   lack of groundwater wells and monitoring networks, (2) complex geology especially in karst [SOURCE: Page_51]

1122   regions (e.g., Hartmann et al., 2014; Tobin and Schwartz, 2016), and (3) the complexity of [SOURCE: Page_51]

1123   subsurface flows in mountain regions (e.g., Knowles and Cayan, 2004; Tague and Grant, [SOURCE: Page_51]

1124   2009; Millares et al., 2009; Godsey et al., 2014). A comprehensive review on the different [SOURCE: Page_51]

1125   groundwater mechanisms and the importance of snow in groundwater recharge under [SOURCE: Page_51]

1126   projected warming scenarios over the western US can be found in the work of Meixner et al., [SOURCE: Page_51]

1127   (2016). [SOURCE: Page_51]

1128             The link between snowmelt and groundwater spring discharge at the mesoscale in [SOURCE: Page_51]

1129   Mediterranean regions was addressed by using hydrochemical analysis. Studies on [SOURCE: Page_51]

1130   experimental snow-influenced micro- to mesoscale basins in Sierra Nevada California [SOURCE: Page_51]

1131   (Taylor et al., 2001; Friedman et al., 2002; Rademacher et al., 2005; Huth et al., 2004; Shaw [SOURCE: Page_51]

1132   et al., 2014), Oregon (Palmer et al., 2007), Serra da Estrela Mountain Portugal (Carreira et [SOURCE: Page_51]

1133   al., 2011), Sierra Nevada Spain (Fernández Chacón et al., 2010), and the Southern Italian [SOURCE: Page_51]

1134   Alps (Penna et al., 2015) demonstrated the potentials for using stable isotopic analysis for [SOURCE: Page_51]

1135   hydrograph separation between snow-fed, rain-fed and groundwater-fed sources and to [SOURCE: Page_51]

1136   investigate flow paths (Bales et al., 2006), and the evolution of snowmelt (Taylor et al., [SOURCE: Page_51]

1137   2001). Tracer tests were used to (1) separate between snowmelt runoff (including rain on [SOURCE: Page_51]

1138   snow) (Williams et al., 2001), subsurface flow and fall storm runoff (Liu et al., 2013; Perrot [SOURCE: Page_51]

1139   et al., 2014) and shallow evapotranspired groundwater from groundwater sources (Shaw et [SOURCE: Page_51]

1140   al., 2014), and (2) investigate meltwater-driven surface runoff and catchment transit time [SOURCE: Page_51]

49 [SOURCE: Page_51]

1141   (e.g., McGuire and McDonnell, 2010). General chemical analyses also provided reliable [SOURCE: Page_52]

1142   information on the state of water residence time in springs (Rademacher et al., 2005). The [SOURCE: Page_52]

1143   karst aquifers are of particular relevance in the Mediterranean region since they represent a [SOURCE: Page_52]

1144   key source of freshwater supply for the people living in the Mediterranean basin (Doummar [SOURCE: Page_52]

1145   et al., 2014). The estimation of groundwater recharge from snowmelt in karst regions had [SOURCE: Page_52]

1146   been limited to mid and low latitude micro-scale snow-influenced mountainous regions with [SOURCE: Page_52]

1147   studies in southern Europe Spain (Andreo et al., 2004), Italy (Allocca et al., 2014), Greece [SOURCE: Page_52]

1148   (Novel et al., 2007), and the eastern Mediterranean regions of Mount Lebanon (Bakalowicz et [SOURCE: Page_52]

1149   al., 2007). A pioneering study in the mid-altitude mountain region in Crete showed promise [SOURCE: Page_52]

1150   for simulating the contribution of snowmelt to karst hydrosystems by coupling a karstic [SOURCE: Page_52]

1151   model and an energy balance snow model (Kourgialas et al., 2010). [SOURCE: Page_52]

1152          New opportunities for the separation between SWE, surface water reservoir storage, [SOURCE: Page_52]

1153   soil moisture, ET, and changes in groundwater storage have been made possible using the [SOURCE: Page_52]

1154   GRACE mission (Famiglietti et al., 2011; Scanlon et al., 2012) and finer-resolution land- [SOURCE: Page_52]

1155   surface models (Singh et al., 2015). The use of global position system (GPS) vertical land [SOURCE: Page_52]

1156   motion observations in the California Central Valley (Ouellette et al., 2013; Argus et al., [SOURCE: Page_52]

1157   2014; Boniface et al., 2015) and Oregon (Fu et al., 2015) seems to show potential for [SOURCE: Page_52]

1158   estimating terrestrial water storage while accounting for snow accumulation and melt. While [SOURCE: Page_52]

1159   these studies are encouraging, they are still limited to the regional scale due to the coarse [SOURCE: Page_52]

1160   resolution of the GRACE observations (approximately 300 km). [SOURCE: Page_52]

1161   7. Conclusion [SOURCE: Page_52]

1162          The review of 620 papers published between 1913 and 2016 demonstrated that the [SOURCE: Page_52]

1163   science behind snowpack dynamics (energy and mass fluxes) and hydrological process in [SOURCE: Page_52]

1164   Mediterranean mountain regions is well developed. The number of studies that are dedicated [SOURCE: Page_52]

50 [SOURCE: Page_52]

1165   to the snow in Mediterranean regions also reflects the societal importance of the topic in the [SOURCE: Page_53]

1166   context of climate change, economic development and population growth. The use of [SOURCE: Page_53]

1167   indicators helped in highlighting major snow hydrologic processes in Mediterranean [SOURCE: Page_53]

1168   mountains. In specific areas, such as in identifying major drivers for snowpack dynamics, it [SOURCE: Page_53]

1169   was difficult to draw definitive conclusions given the variety of approaches (e.g., different [SOURCE: Page_53]

1170   major drivers and methods). Our classification of Mediterranean mountains encompasses a [SOURCE: Page_53]

1171   large range of geologic and physiographic conditions. Despite the fact that the theory behind [SOURCE: Page_53]

1172   hydrologic processes in mountains is well established, drawing a common conclusion [SOURCE: Page_53]

1173   remains difficult beyond the case studies because the limited number of studies in this area of [SOURCE: Page_53]

1174   research area and the variety of approaches used (e.g., different models). [SOURCE: Page_53]

1175          Mediterranean snow-influenced regions are marked by a high inter- and intra-annual [SOURCE: Page_53]

1176   climate variability that shapes up most of the hydrologic processes. As a result, the snow [SOURCE: Page_53]

1177   depth, snow density, and snow water equivalent exhibit high inter- and intra-annual variance. [SOURCE: Page_53]

1178   In addition, the snowpack is affected by higher densification rates compared to other climate [SOURCE: Page_53]

1179   regions. The snowpack energy and mass balances are dominated by radiation fluxes, which [SOURCE: Page_53]

1180   account for most of the energy available for melt. The contribution of sensible and latent heat [SOURCE: Page_53]

1181   fluxes to ablation becomes prominent at the end of the snowmelt season. Snow sublimation [SOURCE: Page_53]

1182   is more pronounced in the high-elevation zones, whereas snowmelt dominates the warmer, [SOURCE: Page_53]

1183   low to mid-elevation regions. The role of snow metamorphism (grain size and albedo) on the [SOURCE: Page_53]

1184   melt onset is still an open field of research. There is also room for improving snow mapping [SOURCE: Page_53]

1185   in forests and assessing the impact of absorbed impurities on radiative exchange at the snow [SOURCE: Page_53]

1186   surface. In particular, the snowpack in the Mediterranean basin is exposed to the deposition [SOURCE: Page_53]

1187   of mineral dust from the surrounding desert areas in Middle East and North Africa. [SOURCE: Page_53]

1188          In Mediterranean mountain regions snowmelt are exposed to (1) periods of low [SOURCE: Page_53]

1189   precipitation or high temperature causing “snow drought” (Cooper et al., 2016), (2) [SOURCE: Page_53]

51 [SOURCE: Page_53]

1190   heatwaves, (3) rain on snow events, and (4) and dust deposition on the snowpack. Increasing [SOURCE: Page_54]

1191   air temperatures will lead to a shift in precipitation regime with elevation (i.e., rain to snow [SOURCE: Page_54]

1192   and increase in rain-on-snow events). The dust deposition tends to enhance the snow melt by [SOURCE: Page_54]

1193   increasing the radiative exchange due to the decrease of snow albedo. Understanding [SOURCE: Page_54]

1194   snowmelt sensitivity to these climate variables is of primary importance to improving our [SOURCE: Page_54]

1195   knowledge on the hydrologic processes and water resources system responses in mountain [SOURCE: Page_54]

1196   regions and downstream areas. [SOURCE: Page_54]

1197          In situ networks are often too sparse given the aforementioned spatial variability and [SOURCE: Page_54]

1198   most of the monitoring stations only monitor few meteorological variables needed to solve [SOURCE: Page_54]

1199   the energy budget of the snowpack. The increasing availability of remote sensing data, [SOURCE: Page_54]

1200   especially in the visible domain, has enabled scientific breakthroughs, such as the [SOURCE: Page_54]

1201   reconstruction of the SWE at the mountain scale with a decametric resolution for the last 30 [SOURCE: Page_54]

1202   years (Margulis et al., 2016). Limitations associated with passive microwave and radar are [SOURCE: Page_54]

1203   physically linked to the band width and are likely to remain in the near future. Moreover, [SOURCE: Page_54]

1204   airborne techniques (e.g., NASA’s ASO (Painter et al., 2016)) are an option for acquiring [SOURCE: Page_54]

1205   highly detailed and direct estimates of HS and SWE once their high operational costs are [SOURCE: Page_54]

1206   reduced. [SOURCE: Page_54]

1207          Data assimilation portability to different regions is hindered by the reliance on larger [SOURCE: Page_54]

1208   data sets as input variables. In regions with limited ground-based observations, model outputs [SOURCE: Page_54]

1209   can be biased by an increased uncertainty. Similarly, the reliance on multiple other [SOURCE: Page_54]

1210   technologies (e.g., ground-based and remote sensing observations) makes the system highly [SOURCE: Page_54]

1211   dependent on the simultaneous availability of data from all systems (Guan et al., 2013a). The [SOURCE: Page_54]

1212   results using reanalysis of remote sensing data (Margulis et al., 2016) and physically based [SOURCE: Page_54]

1213   simulations run using the mesoscale WRF model (e.g., Caldwell et al., 2009; Wayand et al., [SOURCE: Page_54]

1214   2013) seem to provide better results of SWE in regions with limited station data. One of the [SOURCE: Page_54]

52 [SOURCE: Page_54]

1215   promising areas for research is the development of combined products, which include ground [SOURCE: Page_55]

1216   observations and remotely sensed data through data assimilation in snowpack models. [SOURCE: Page_55]

1217          Conducting snow measurements in less monitored regions such as Lebanon's [SOURCE: Page_55]

1218   mountains and the Atlas Mountains is required, and these investigations can make use of the [SOURCE: Page_55]

1219   extended knowledge gained from other Mediterranean regions. In the meantime, the models [SOURCE: Page_55]

1220   based on climate reanalysis and remote sensing data are more easily transferrable. Snow [SOURCE: Page_55]

1221   density and SWE spatiotemporal variability can be estimated, with an acceptable accuracy, [SOURCE: Page_55]

1222   using a few years of ground observation (e.g., Anderton et al., 2004; Mizukami and Perica, [SOURCE: Page_55]

1223   2008). Maintaining the existing in situ network is critical for monitoring the snowpack [SOURCE: Page_55]

1224   response to climate change. Free access to open software and snow and meteorological data [SOURCE: Page_55]

1225   in the western US has allowed an intense development of the snow science in this region. [SOURCE: Page_55]

1226   This review suggests that a similar open data, open code policy is the path forward for other [SOURCE: Page_55]

1227   Mediterranean regions. Although snow models, hydrological models and land surface models [SOURCE: Page_55]

1228   are increasingly distributed as open source software, data assimilation codes are less [SOURCE: Page_55]

1229   available. There is still need for more collaboration in terms of standardizing and sharing [SOURCE: Page_55]

1230   data. It is important, that snow observations, measurements and analysis are documented and [SOURCE: Page_55]

1231   archived in online snow data repositories (Kinar and Pomeroy, 2015) to serve as a source of [SOURCE: Page_55]

1232   information and provide data that can be used for research purposes and intercomparison [SOURCE: Page_55]

1233   projects. [SOURCE: Page_55]

1234          Advancing our understanding of hydrological processes in Mediterranean mountains [SOURCE: Page_55]

1235   is partly hindered by the sparse meteorological stations and hydrological gauging networks, [SOURCE: Page_55]

1236   and the large uncertainty in key variables, such as the stream flow in headwaters catchments [SOURCE: Page_55]

1237   (Avanzi et al., 2014; Lundquist et al., 2015b; Raleigh et al., 2016). In addition, there remain [SOURCE: Page_55]

1238   challenges in taking advantage of these advances in catchment hydrology because the [SOURCE: Page_55]

1239   response of the streamflow to the snowmelt is modulated by other hydrologic processes (ET, [SOURCE: Page_55]

53 [SOURCE: Page_55]

1240   infiltration, groundwater flow). These processes are more strongly influenced by the [SOURCE: Page_56]

1241   subsurface properties (soil, geology), and hence, they are less easily constrained by current [SOURCE: Page_56]

1242   observational networks, remote sensing and modeling technologies. Closing on the water [SOURCE: Page_56]

1243   balance in Mediterranean mountain regions seems to be only feasible when these processes [SOURCE: Page_56]

1244   are solved simultaneously. Understanding of the connection between snowmelt, streamflow, [SOURCE: Page_56]

1245   evapotranspiration, and groundwater flow in snow-dominated regions is an open field for [SOURCE: Page_56]

1246   future research (e.g., Jefferson et al., 2008; Liu et al., 2013; Godsey et al., 2014; Jepsen et al., [SOURCE: Page_56]

1247   2016a). Furthermore, there is a need to integrate human processes, such as reservoir [SOURCE: Page_56]

1248   management and irrigation, to investigate the vulnerability of the water resources under [SOURCE: Page_56]

1249   global change scenarios (e.g., Maurer et al., 2007; López-Moreno et al., 2008b; Viviroli et al., [SOURCE: Page_56]

1250   2011; Anghileri et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_56]

1251          Snowpack response to climate variability and change remains one of the critical issues [SOURCE: Page_56]

1252   in Mediterranean mountains that motivated many of the reviewed studies (e.g., Guan et al., [SOURCE: Page_56]

1253   2012, 2013b). Projected scenarios indicate a marked warming and increased dryness in [SOURCE: Page_56]

1254   Mediterranean regions, which will amplify the transition from a snow-dominated to a rain- [SOURCE: Page_56]

1255   dominated in mid elevation watersheds, and reduce the seasonal SWE and its persistence. [SOURCE: Page_56]

1256   These snow-related changes may have broad implications on evapotranspiration, groundwater [SOURCE: Page_56]

1257   recharge and runoff in many Mediterranean catchments. Mediterranean snow dominated [SOURCE: Page_56]

1258   basins are vulnerable to the increase in temperature and recurring dry periods. The [SOURCE: Page_56]

1259   sustainability of the water system, in the lowland regions, requires better understanding of the [SOURCE: Page_56]

1260   seasonal snow water storage and release as well as the quantification of uncertainties [SOURCE: Page_56]

1261   associated to the projected climate change, population growth, and land-use changes on the [SOURCE: Page_56]

1262   hydrologic responses of mountainous basins (e.g., Barnett et al., 2008; Morán-Tejeda et al., [SOURCE: Page_56]

1263   2014). These challenges remain partially understood and warrant future research to anticipate [SOURCE: Page_56]

1264   their management in the coming decades. [SOURCE: Page_56]

54 [SOURCE: Page_56]

1265   8. Acknowledgments [SOURCE: Page_57]

1266           This publication was made possible through support provided by the French National [SOURCE: Page_57]

1267   Research Institute for Sustainable Development (IRD). We thank the editor and two [SOURCE: Page_57]

1268   anonymous reviewers for their review comments and suggestions that have improved the [SOURCE: Page_57]

1269   presentation of this paper. [SOURCE: Page_57]

2198   •   snow distribution and densification rates are marked by high intra-annual variability 2199   •   snow cover persistence is mainly controlled by precipitation and elevation 2200   •   snowmelt is driven by radiative fluxes 2201   •   heat flux contribution to snowmelt increases during heat waves and rain-on-snow events 2202   •   snow sublimation is an important component of snow ablation in high-elevation regions 2203 [SOURCE: Page_80]
